The Princess Royal, who has inherited all her grandmother's love of the Highlands, is staying with the Duke of Fife, and her daughters at Mar Lodge until the end of October. Her Royal Highness and the Duke rarely entertain large partiee at Mar Lodge, although I the House contains more than 120 spacious rooms, but I the King and the Prince of Wales will probably be i guests there when the first of the deer drives in Mar Forest takes place at the end of this month. Royalty and Open-air Life.—The Dnchees of Connaught, with Princess Patricia—who certainly inherits her parents love of the simple out-door life, has been devoting much of her time lately to golf, taking its usual no heed of the weather. Despite her Jove of open-air life however, the Duchess, like all Gerrnam Princesses, is unceasing in the care of her home and family, and both at Bagshot Park, and Clarence House, household arrangements run upon oiled wheels and the utmost comfort prevails. The Duchess of Conuaught is a keen collector of old furniture, rare china and odd treasures, which she has brought mostly from foreign lands and distributed about her homes. Women's Golfing Dress.—Cream is the colour chosen by the modish feminine golfer just now. Her stort skirt, made of aide workmanlike blanket serge, smart knitted coat, and tam-o'-shanter' are all in cream, not a hint of colour appearing about the whale costume. A more serviceable scheme, nevertheless, is the navy serge skirt, the knitted coat or jersey, tam-o'-shanter' and leather hem to the skirt being nut brown. Red is little seen upon the golf course, which is a matter for regret, the note it affords, there, as upon the river, being artistic and effective. Dress in Spain.—So far, Queen Victoria Eugenie has not followed the fashion beloved by Spanish women, of wearing black very frequently. Her Majesty still keeps to her favourite white, pale blue, and pale pink toilettes, and save now and again in the evening rarely dons black at all. As the average Spanish Complexion is pale, indeed often waxen white it is somewhat remark- able that black should prove so becoming to it, but the fact remains, and is not overlooked by the stately graceful women of the country. Combinations in Colours and Fabrics.—ln the world of colours, a very effective alliance is that of the dull soft shades of terracotta and green, while another is that of olive green, and pale pinkish huff. Two other shades which at the moment are working well together, are the rich purple and red tones of the China aster. The fashion for trimming light weight fabrics with heavy ones, promises to continue, and withont doubt it is a particularly graceful one. Of course we should see to it that the heavy fabric is not too heavy for the lighter one, and therefore likely to tear Away from it, which, by the way, is a point dressmakers do not often very carefully consider. Japanese Umbrellas.—Real Japanese nmbrellas made of paper, and carried out in vo^y subtle coleurs and strange designs, have had this summer quite a vogue at eeaside resorts, both at home and abroad. When seen with the simplest of hats and gowns, the effect has not been unpleasing, but with smart dressy toilettes it must bse confessed the idea has often made decidedly for the theatrical. The Directoire Directoire stick is new affected by extreme fashionists conspicuons in the dress of that period. Very long; and usually of ebony or jet black, the Directoire stick is fitted with a gold headed cross piece, fashioned like a snuff-box ; and which is used as a receptacle for pastilles and bon-bons, iqatead of snuff as in the olden times. Something like a mild sensation has been created on the few occasions upon which these sticks have made their appearance in London lately, and every device will be used to make them fashionable during the autumn. Then and Now.—Tho International Council of Women' which assembled this week at Geneva, reminds us how far we have advanced in the emancipation of woman,' since the times when she was commolityliiated as the ! slave and chattel of man. One of the London papers which reprints items from its news columns, of a 1 hundred years ego; recently furnished evidence as to how well-founded was the Frenchman's idea that it was' asommon thirg for Englishmen to sell their wives. It recorded in one week, in 1808, four instances of this kind in London, one of which took place in Smithfield Market, where the wife, with the customary appendage' of a halter, was sold for three shillings. A few shillings was the average figure in such contracts, but often it was only liquor, and in one of these particular cases the wife was told for a pint of gin, and agallon of porter. on which the husband get dont:, fell into the deck, and was drowned.—Partieulars also appeared in the same issue of a min who made a bet that he could drink a , butt of porter in six dries that is, 18 gallons per day, and he performed the clisgustieg feat with apparent ease."—Evidently it may be said of the lower orders of a hundred years ago, as of the ancient Britons, that " manners they Lad none, and their customs were beastly." The "Gibson Girl" Way.—Marriage has deprived Mr. Sevraore Hicks of the services of yet another of his " Gibson Girls " in the person of aliss Elsie Kay, who was married last week to 51 r. Ronald McAndrew, eon of a wealthy shipbuilder of Liverpool. This makes the tenth of Mr. Hick's "Gibson Girls" who have suddenly left the stage for tbe ancient but still popular institution of matrimony, all of whom have married raen of great wealth, end in several instances also of high social position. In years gone by popular actresses had a way of attaining free advertisement by not infre- quently losing their jewelsahut now it eeems they lose their hearts and it would appear that one of the. easiest ways of doipg this is be engaged by Mr. Seymore Hicks to play in the attractive rote of a "Gibson Girl." Linen " Shelves" for Boxes.—A capital plan when making a round of short visits is, to place between each set of garments packed into a box, a length of soft linen, rather solidly hemmed to the size of the I receptable. Thus, eft" a layer of underwear, a linen "shelf "should be arran gad, then would come perchance a layer of petticoats, a layer of skirts, a layer of hand- kerchiefs, gloves, ties, belts, and collars, a layer of blouses, Sm., between each of which a "shelf " of linen tbculd be laid. This device saves infinite trouble, as each layer can be lifted out without the bother, muddle, end loss of time of unpacking. Many women sew little tapes at the four corners of each " shelf " upon which they mark the name of the articles which lie above, still further simplifying the task of finding things in a hurry without unpacking.

THE 110DE8 AS THEY ABE.

Neglige gowns are close clinging, with scanty drapery, somewhat too long for comfort, due tb the fact ,that they emanate from Paris, and that most Frenchwomen have plump figures, and a trained gown gives an appearance of height. Some of the clinging satin knd gauze gowns are very surprising, and they are worn in Paris by people in society. It is necessary nowadays to study one's figure; otherwise one is apt to be ridiculous. The revival of Empire fashions comes from Paris, and while not unbecoming to stout figures disguises too slight ones.

TO-DAT'S RECIPE.

PIGEONS EN SITRPRISE.—PIuck and draw the pigeons, allowing one for each person. Blanch thorn for a few minutes in boiling salted water. Take them out, and into th 3 same water plunge an equal number of fine cabbage lettuce. Let these boil five minutes; then lift out, rinse in cold water, and squeeze in a cloth. Cut them down the centre to' open, without breaking the outer leaves; make a hollow inside and sprinkle with herbs and seasoning and the chopped gib- lets of the pigeons. Then place in the hollow thus prepared one pigeoh and close up so as to !make the lettuce appear as if, untouched. Tie securely with thread and set the lettuces in a kstewpan, and cook with broth enough to just !cover them for fully an hour. Drain them and remove tho threads; then serve with a thickened !sauce made from the broth, & yolk of egg, butter ' and flour. PRETTY AFTERNOON DRESS.

For satin-faced cloth in a delie'ate shade of cinnamon is the afternoon dress shewn in our sketch intended. Down the front of the dress there is a plain panel, reaching from the yoke to the foot of the skirt. This is embroidered a little near the top, and is trimmed with a double row of buttons covered with glacti silk the same shade as the material. The bodice is arranged in folds

layer the shoulders, and trimmed with small ball fringe, the wide sleeves being also trimmed with fringe. The guimpe is of fine net, drawn up to a little round yoke of thicker lace. The full sleeves are of net, and finished with little turn- back cuffs of lace.. Long close-fitting net, cuffs could be, added if wished. The folded sash is of cretie-cle-ohine to match the cloth, and it is tied in a bow at the side and the ends finished with tassels.

TILE LADY PIIRSED.

lOne of the Cunard liners has, it seems, a lady purser. During a recent trip they promoted their stenographer to be assistant purser, and it / is reported that some other steamship lines have announced that they mean also to appoint women as pursers or assistant pursers. There has never seemed any reason, comments the travel editor of the ()wen, why women of the right stamp should not be pursers. They would have to be very good sailors, but, then, many women are that. On some lines, to be a good linguist would be a groat advantage. Comment- ing on the innovation, a well-known society occupied with extending the field of women's work in general remarks that a woman purser _seemed to them an excellent idea; while the secretary of the Travellers' Aid Society for Women and Girls', who is perhaps in a better position to know exactly what a purser's life I means, thinks a woman purser should be middle- : aged, and should only be concerned with .the ; women passengers. One purser r knew under- took the most manifold duties, from getting up fancy-dress balls and advising on tho ladies dresses for them, to decorating the dinner. tables With flowers and plants, if possible; and with elaborate devices of coloured baby ribbon, if the ship were too far from a port for flowers and plants to be still fresh.

NATTIER - BLUE CASHMERE FROCK.

Our sketch shows an autumn dress for a young girl. It would be carried out in Nattier-blue cashmere, and would make a charming "best dress" to wear during the coming term. The skirt is arranged with a little fulness at the back of the waist, and there are two tucks above the hem. FASHION AND FAT.

• When fashion demands slender- gracefulness of NV tywen who have to break corset laces to make them- selves even presentab'e, isn't it fottnnate that science steps in and makes their task easy? • - For dieting and exercising science has substituted • pTeasant. wholesome mixture that can be obtained from any chemist, viz.: Ore half-ounce of Marthola, tone ounce of fluid extract of Glycyrrhiza 13,13. ODO ounce of Fire Glycerine 8.P., and Peppermint Water to make six ounces in all. One dessertspoonrul.of this mixture after meals and at bedtime will take off four to seven pounds of fat a week without causing wrinkles, and without inter- fering with the diet or distressing the stomach in the slightest degree.

Cautht in the Act.

At the Borough Police Court on Thursday, William Newton, Friars' Green, was charged with stealing from the yard-of Albert Wright, builder, Martury- street, a brush and three pairs cf hinges. The Chief Constable said prisoner was occasionally ernrloyed by the 1. resecutor, who for some little limo had been missing goods. On Thursday he kept observation, and shot t'y after noon he saw prisorer take the brush and hinges and place them under his coat. He 2CCOE ted him, and prisoner replied: "Don't lock me 'rip. Forgive me this tmae." Prisoner was given in charge of Constable Woollacott. He had been twice previously before the Com t. The magistrates sen- itenced prisoner to a month's hard labour.

GARMIRTR. McDOUGALL'S Patinit SELF-RAISING FLOUR The Examinek Saturday, September 5, 1908. WOMAN'S REALM. ISCIENCE NOTES AND NE-WS. PHOTOGRAPHIC NOTES. By —Veronica.

MICROBES IN MILK.

Some ;interesting experiments with milk are reported in the Journal of the American Medical Association. Milk of the purest de- scription, when put into the cold-storage room containing only a few hundred bac- teria to the cubic centimetre, shews at the end of - a week a pronounced growth of or- ganisms, and after five or six weeks the num- ber reaches hundreds of millions in the cubic centimetre, even though the tempera- ture may be so low that the Milk assumes a semi-solid mass of ice crystals. It is note:- worthy that such milk has no odour or taste , to indicate the presence of the bacteria.

RAT BRAINS LIKE kAN'S.

In an address- to the American Philoso- phical Society, Professor Henry. Donaldson I said that the results of researches made by him shew that the growth of the brain and spinal cord in the albino rat closely approxi- mates the development of "these organs in man. His conclusion is that the rat -may be used in laboratory experiments which apply to man, and for, which the brain of the human species would be available only at rare times. That the eubject of his remarks might not be confused with the *common, or cellar, variety of rodent, Professor Donald- son said the albino ra,t, "is a fancy strain d ihe Norway or grey. rat."

STARS BEYOND SIGHT.

When the air is damp and warm a new iron surface will corrode . cry quickly. Ex- posure for a single day is enough to mark a nail here and there with specks of rust, easily removed by touch. However closely one might observe, the nail from the first moment of its exposure. it would, says the New York Tribune, be impossible for him to see the change taking place in his colour. The eye would tire of watching long before a grain of rust would come -lido. view. New if, instead of the eye, •so feeble and so easily fatigued, we have something itore sensitive to light and not subject to weariness, we can at once indirectly and very greatly extend the field of human. vision. Such a substitute is -the photographic plate, when it is treated with newly-devised com- pounds, on which- rays •

TOO FAINT TO BE DETECTED OTHERWISE -can imprint themselves. Besides its ex- quisite se neitiveness to: light, this plate has another advantage over the eye. We may look directly at a distant star; but if its lay is too weak to excite vision, our gaze is in vain, no matter how long we may be able to maintain it. Not so with the plate. The rays that fall on it, no matter how faint they may be, steadily increase their effect from hour to hour. until they yield a distinct image. AB this has been applied in a wonderful way to the study of the, heavens. With every improvement in the power of telescopes, astronomers have brought into their ken nebulae and stars before tanseen. They know that invisible stars must far outnumber the visible ones; for the whole volume of star- light is much greater than the beams re- ceived from the stars within the telescope's range. Their belief, based on past observa- tion, is that space beyond vision is ever ready to reveal -a

===== 0002601_19080905_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW WEALVI OF WORLD

to any new power of -exploration. Precisely such a power is conferred by the sensitive plate. : When it is attached to a telescope whose lenses are by suitable tnychanism kept constantly directed at a, particular point in the sky, the astronomer is able vastly to ex- tend the diameter of the visible universe. Orbs 60 remote ,as to he invislble to the keenest observer, in the course or a night form a. clear picture in the camera. In se- curing pictures of particular heavenly- bodies photography is quite as important 116 in mak- ing charts of the stars. Professor Lowell has declared that the camera—in- half an hour has given him more knowledge of the sur- face of Mars than he would have been able' to obtain in five years' observation. The satellite of Neptune, though invisible- in the telescope on account of -its proximity to 'the planet, has been photographed in every part of ktg orbit. It was, reserved for the photo- grapher, by a fine 'stretch of audacity, to pompel the- sun to take its own portrait, and to do it in the fraction of a second.

SUGAR VAPOUR AS ANTISEPTIC.

The custom of burning sugar in a sick- room is very current among all classes in France, but up to the present has been re- garded by scientists as • one of those harm- leas and useless practices which are rather tolerated than insisted upon by the medical profession. But M. Trillat, of the Pasteur Institute, new assures, us that formic alde- hyde is given off by burning sugar, and is one of the most antiseptic gases known. Five grams of sugar having been burnt under a ten-litre ,bell glass, the vapour was allowed to cool. Vials containing the bacilli of typhoid, tuberculosis, carbon, .&c., were then introduced. Within half an hour every microbe had succumbed. Again, if • sugar be ;burnt in a closed vessel' containing rotten eggs or putrid meat the disagreeable smell disappears. .M. Trillat affirms that the formic aldehyde combines with the gases given off by the putrid, animal, ma.tter and renders them_ inodorous.

BEAUTIFUL WARRINGTON.

Nfy dear Beautiful Warrington Children,— The holidays are over ! I hope you have had happy times in the country or at the seaside and now you are back again at school with your minds filled with happy memories of days spent in close touch with Nature. I should like to hear from you what was the most wonderful or beautiful sight in Nature which you saw during your holidays. A wonderful sunrise at sea impressed me most. I had spent, all night on the deck of the steamer. Very quiet was the ship, its hundreds of passengers sleeping in their berths below. The night was beautifully fine. The stars faded away one by one, until there were only the morning star and the thiu silvery streak of the waning moon, shining in the sky. Quickly the darkness passed away and the whole sky was covered with gray silvery clouds. From the sea shafts of gplden light shot up, until the rosy hue of morning adorned sky and sea. Night had fled and out of eternity another blue day had dawned. Children, study the sky for often its beauty surpasses the loveliness of earth. I wonder if you have thought much of our Society while you have been away. I have been far away in "La Belle Franee " and I kept my eyes open to see if I could get any hints for our members. Most of in time was spent in an old town built one high rock standing out in the sea. The town was originally built' by the English and the strong walls and some of the old buildings are standing to-day. Though the town was old and quaint, and very ,interesting, I looked in vain for a garden. There were no back-yards to.convert into beauty spots, in fact there were no spaces, back or front, to make into gardens. Yet I saw signs which allowed that Granville people loved Vother Earth's gifts, just as much as the members of our Society. I was walking on the walls of the old town when I saw a sight which made me stop. The house was half In ruins and very dirty, yet somebody lived there who loved bright pretty plants for the window was gay with nasturtiums and sweet peas. You'll never guess what they were growing in ! There was no window-sill to hold a window-box, but fastened to the side of the window was an old tin can. In this were planted the nasturtiums and sweet peas, and with a wonderful con- trivance of umbrella ribs and wire, the plants were trained all over the window until its dirtiness was hidden. Pr= another window hung carnations far more beautiful than those which won the prizes at Stockton Heath or even Warrington flower Shows. I visited two French Schools in the town. I was pleased with the buildings and the playgrounds. The playground was nicely laid out with flower-beds ; tall trees along the side-walks made a welcome shade. The boys, being rougher, had no flower-beds in their yard, but fifteen trees planted in the form of three sides of a square, made it shady and pleasant. Their garden was arranged along one side of the school and it was exceedingly pretty, the roses and masses of heliotrope giving out a most lovely perfume. I wonder when every playgrot.nd in Warrington will have its flower beds and shady trees ? it is nice to know that Beamont schools have saoh beautiful grounds, that the scholars can have a gardea party •, but what is beiia done to beautify the playgrounds and fronts of the twenty old schools? Children, I think you will have to make an effort and see if you cannot follow the good example of Beamont School and have a garden day next year. 1 was very pleased to helr from the members of the Sinith-street School branch; and hope they will con- tinue ip their efforts to beautify this dear old town.— Yours affectionately,

V.M.T.

BEAUTIFUL WARRINGTON SOCIETY.

THIRD ANNUAL FLOWER SHOW.

. We are progressing—somewhat slowly, it must be admitted— towards the Beautiful Warrington ideal and the interest of the townspeople in the growth of flowers and the beautifying of the stree!s was never greater than at the present time. Quietly but never- theless successfully, the Beautiful Warrington Society is pursuing its work, and many of the pretty gardens and choice window diiplays of flowers which tendAo relieve the drabness of the town are the direct out- come of its propaganda. Saturday witnessed' the holding cf the third annual exhibition of flowers and plants promoted by the Society and the Drillilall pre- sented a scene of beauty which at once appealed, to the eye of the visitor. Notwithstanding . the grimy atmosphere, it is evident that some of the best of flowers can be grown in Warrington, and Saturday's show Was a credit to all concerned. It was undoubtedly a great improvement on previous displays and the exhibits had been artistically arranged with a view to getting the best possible effect from the material available. The school children's section was perhaps the most interesting and showed th it the wot k is making good headway amongst the rising generation. A new Lecture was an exhibition of pressed leaves and flowers. The attractiveness...el the show was much enhanced by the non•compcting exhibits which included some very fine groups of plants The following is a list of those who sent collections: —The Copolation of

Warrington, Mr. and Mrs. Lyon, Mr. and Mrs. Long, Mrs. Stanton, Mr. J. Fairhurst, Mrs. L. Greening, Mr. t Ward, Mr. W. Powell, Mrs. Fords, Mr. Gordon Roberts, Mr. Corbett, Mr. IL licpe, Mr. F. Brody, Mr A. Broady, Mr. Bonehill, Mr. J. Ryan, , Mr. Joseph Blinston• ana Alderman Bolton (who forwarded some exquisite orchids) A unique exhibit was sent by Mr. John Fairhurst. It consisted of flowers and plants suitable for growing out of doors in Warrington, and the following list will doubtless prove of interest to gardening enthusiasts: = Aralia f White Rock) single and doable, Aralia SiebolJii, Aralia Spinosa, Antirrhinum, Aubrietia, Anemone Japonica, Balsam (common), Campanula (three varieties), Coreopsis, Carnations, Centaurea, Chrysanthemum (early flowering), Chrysanthemum (maximum), Delphinium, Dahlias, single, double, cactus and pompom.; Erigeron, Fuchsia (three vat ieties), Forget-ine-nots, Gaillardia, Golden rod (dwarf and tall), Gladiola, Hydrangea panicidata, llelenium' Lilium (three varitiss), Mignonette, Nas- turtium, Nigalla (Love.ima-mist)," Peiwinkle '(two varieties), Phlox, Sunflowers, &dam, Sweet Williams, Salvia, Saxifrlge, Prince's Feather and other varieties, Sweet Peas, Pansy, Thrift (Sea Pink), lea Week Stook, Tobacco, and Violas (in 12 varieties).

===== 0002601_19080905_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
THE OPENING CERENIONV.

The show was opened by Lady Greenall in the presence of a fairly large gathering. The Rev. Canon Stevenson (chairman of the Executive Committee) presided and there were also present on the platform : —Sir Gilbert Greenall, the Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Smethurst), the Rev. J. Collins, the Rev. Father Whittle, Mrs. Garnett, Mr. tnd Mrs. C. E. Parker, Alderman Bennett, Mr. William Silcock, Mr. E. J. Hall, Mr. John Fairhurst and Mr. J. Longland. The CH VIRMAN said that was the third flower show which had been organised by the society, and he thought that even a casual glance around the room would cause the audience to admit that it was not only the third show, but that they had gone one better than the two previous ones. (Hear, bear.) They, had an increase of over 30 entries, end it would have been greater, especi- ally with regard to cut flowers, if it had not been for the high winds and heavy rains which had been experi- enced during the past few days which, of course, had dashed the outside flowers considerably. One of the features they were most anxious about- in connection with their work was the prizes given for the

BEST BACK GARDENS. They wished to show people that the back yards of Warrington could be made, as some of them had been made, really attractive places in which a man and his wife could sit on Sunday afternoons and enjoy them- selves almost as much as if they were in the country. It might be of interest to them if he read the report of the judges with regard to the gardens. "We found the total entries are about equal to last year, being 51 as against 53. The number of exhibitors this year is 43, last year 40. Of the exhibitors of last year only 21 have entered for, this year's competitions, and of these we consider that more than half have not kept up to ..their previous standard. This diminution of effort is to be regretted. Some of the new exhibits are models of what small back gardens ought to be, as not only were they nicely laid out, but the quality of the plants grown was vary good, showing that even in towns flowers can be grown successfully under certain conditions. We would point out that there are again no entries from the Bewsey and St. John's Wards' and only one from Howley Ward and two from Orford Ward. We think that considering the increased interest in window-boxes more than 14 entries from the whole town might have been expected, and ought to be forthcoming another year. We feel that the best efforts of the society could be directed by the owners of houses, or groups of owners, with the assist- anee of the Ward Committees, interesting the whola of the tenants in such streets as lend themselves to treat- ment. The conditions of site, f-ize and situation of gs •dens vary so mu3h as to render competition unfair, and street prizes would be the best way to meet such difflculties." (Applause). Another important branch was the

===== 0002601_19080905_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
WORK AMONGSP CHILDREN.

In the children lay their hopes for the future awl he was happy to say that, thanks to the efforle of the teachers in the schools, the juvenile branch had done most excellent work, In their report the judges (Messrs. James Ryan and W. H. Woodcock) said:—" In all we visited 24 departments of the schools, and 918 plants were submitted to us as egainst 1,351 last year. The large decrease in numbers was said by the teachers to be due in part to some of the children having seen other competitors' pleats, and, thinkine their own inferior, did not care to stage theirs. Whilst there was this decrease in numbers we are glad to report that the efforts of the juvenile gardeners and the excellent condition of their plants were most encouraging. The exhibits in a great majority of cases bore evidence of great care whilst it was noted that there were fewer failures than in preceding years ; there was in fact a general all-round excellence far in advance of the last two years.We trust the teachers will continue to up keep the enthusiasm of their pupils and that next year will bring about an increase in numbers, as this year has shown an advance in culture and general attention. (Applause.) Canon Stevenson said he should like to add that they had received apologies for non-attendance from their President (Colonel Robsrt Blaceburnel, and Mrs. Blackburne, Mr. and Mrs. J. Charlton Parr, Mr. and Mrs. W. Long (who had sent a beautiful -exhibit), Mr. and Mrs. Henry Greenall and the Rev. C. Harvey Cook. He hoped they would all remember what he thought ought to be their chief thought in connection with that exhibition, and that was that they did not see around them plants which had been grown under favourable conditions. Those who were exhibiting were not the most wealthy people, and they had to contend with atmospheric and other conditions, that in a manufacturing town were not favourable to plant growth, they all trusted that those conditions would improve, but at the present moment there was much to contend with. Hes thought when they looked around land saw the beautiful plants and flowers, and remembered the conditions under which they had been grown, they would understand something of the patience, pluck and enthusiasm of the members of the Society, who had done so well under such trying and difficult conditions. (Applause). Lady GIIEENALL, who was received with applause, snid the pursuit of gardesing was one that was suited to all ages and to both,seses. It also did a very great deal of gdod because not only did it give great pleasure to those who were able to follow the pursuit, but nntold pleasure to their fellow creatures, who through force of circumstances, and often through poverty, were not able to enjoy the advantages which others enjoyed. They could only enjoy these things by the efforts of others, because when they walked down the streets they saw beautiful flowers in the window boxes which must be a pleasere to them and, perhaps, brighten some lives which required a great deal of brightening. (Hear, hear and applause.) She most heartily con- gratulated the society on the results of their efforts, as the exhibition was a very creditable one indeed. As Canon Stevenson bad said, the atmosphere of the town and the weather which had been experienced of late, had been unfavourable to the growth of flowers and plants, and it was therefore, all th 6 more creditable that they had such a magniffeent collection.-(Applause.) The MAYOR, in proposing a vote of (thanks to Lady Greenall for her , presence and for opening the exhibition, said he admitted that the show was a good one, and that the Society was doing a great deal of good in a town like Warrington in entiesvonring to inculcate into the minds of children a love for plants' end flowers, and lie hoped the efforts of the children would be encouraged by the parents—but with a popu- lation of 70,033 that room ought to have been packed with flowers and plants. Their neighbours at Stockton Heath, whenever they had a show, always succeeded id crowding the room in which it was held, and there should have been R similar reeult in their own case. Stockton Heath was giving Warrington the lead in the matter and if they did rest look out it would he a case of " Warrington, neer Stockton Heath," soon. (Laughter.) Mr. C. E. PARKER seconded, and congratulated the society on the continued success of its operatiors. It was somewhat easy to launc)sa scheme like this, but it was more difficult to keep it pine, and generally the third year was the trying year. Canon Stevenson had

UnLY AND A SMOKY TOWN.

That was what- the "Beautiful Warrington Society " had to disabuse people's minds of. He was not sure whether the Health Committee did not consider that smoke was inevitable and that the more smoke meant more prosperity. But that was a great mistake and some day, perhaps, not only the manufacturers, but the Health Committee would learn that manufacturing could be carried on practically without smoke. (Hear, hear, and applause). Isio doubt it would take some time to bring that about. lie knew the diflcul- ties in the way, but they ought to be making more pro- gress in that respect than they were making. ((fear, bear). Referring to the value of example in the work of beautification, Mr. Parker said he had been busy with others in laying out a piece of land in connection with I , "The Gardens Suburbs C 0.," and his fear was that the backs of certain houses which were towards the suburbs I land would be a constant eyesore and that the only way I was to plant a shubbery which, in time, would hide the ' backs of the houses. The people living in the houses, however, seems what was being done, had taken the matter in band and those parts which ho thought at one time would b'e an eyesore would be one of the most pleasant features. (Hear, hear and applause). The resolution was carried with much heartiness. During the afternoon the Stockton Heat. Garden Society orchestra played some capital selectiofilf, under the conductorship of Mr. W. E. Ray.

PATHETIC INCIDENT.

AT THE SERVIAN LEGATION IN LONDON.

I In the palatial establishment of the Serviau Legation I in Pout-street, London, S.W., there was enacted, some little time ago, an incident of peculiar pathos. Madame EUGESTIE GRUM lay ill and so serious was her state that medical men insisted that she should be taken to a connenient hospital. From that institu- tion, however, she was removed at a later date, her case being pronounced incurable. The further facts published below have bean related by M ne. Grate herself, who tells the pathetic story of her fifteen years' trial,

"Fifteen years ago I began to fall languid and depressed," said Madame Grare. "At times fits of dizziness came over me and,. I was unable to sand. Then terrible pains seized Ins in my back and chest. Any exertion caused such palpitation and breathless. nese that to walk was out of the question. From having a ruddy complexion, ray lips become white and blondlesa, and my appearance, deathly, "Through sheer weakthas had to take to my bed. The mere sight of food was distaste- ful, and a good night's rest was unknown, for I only had fitful snatches of sleep. " Medical specialists who were consulted told me to my consternation, that I was con- sumptive, and held out no hope for my recovery. "Then Hemorrhage of the Lungs set in and I lost so much blood that I was even too weak to speak. I was so helpless that my nurses had even to feed me. Doctors advised my zonoval to the hospital, and a specialist said to my husband, 'your wife cannot.posibly live.' I remained in the-hospital three weeks : then I was discharged as incurable and taken home again. "It was on the 23rd February (I well remember the date) I read in a newspaper of a case similar to mine being cured by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for The Seryian Legally*tl P. ale People. I had but lite hope ; however, I took these pills for some little time, and judge of my pleasure when I felt better and gained strength. I continued with Dr. Williams' pink pills and the feelings of weariness and lassitude decreased I could take my meals with enjoyment. I slept every night through, a thing I had not done for many years, and persevering with Dr. Williams' pink pills, My cure was soon complete. I never felt so well as I have been since."

PADGATE. '

WESLEYAN Cur:RCM—On Sunday evening at Padgate Wesleyan Church, the Rev. William Mellor, resident Wesleyan minister in Padgate for several years past, who'is about to retire from the ministry and take up his residence at Ormskirk, preached his last sermon before leaving the ‘Varrington circuit. He took for his text Hebrews xii. 3, and spoke very effectively. REV. W. MELLOR'S DEPARTURE : PRESENTATIONS.— On Tuesday evening, presentations were made to the Rev. William Mellor, Mrs. Mellor and their son, Mr. Hugh Mellor, by the members of the church and con- gregation attached to the Padgate Wesleyan Church. The Rev. William Mellor has been the Wesleyan resident minister of Padgate for the past three years, and Mr. Hugh Mellor, who is an eiticient organist, has on many occasions officiated at the organ and rendered assistance to the choir. The Rev. Win. Mellor who is now being placed on the supernumerary list, his term of office having expired, is with his family about to remove to Ormskirk, and the opportunity was taken by many friends to show their appreciation of his services and also the affection and esteem in which ho and his family are held in the district—Mr. Alderman Bennett presided over a very large audience which assembled in the schoolroom and Dr. Bennett, Messrs. J. E. Richardson, Newton and Duckworth made the formal presentations. -•-The Rev. Wm. lollor was presented with a handsome 8 day clock, Mrs. Mellor with an easy chair and Mr. Hur,h Mellor with a fitted dressing case and smoking cabinet. Felicitous speeches were made suitable to the occasion.

TELE CHURCH INSTITUTE. —Lest Monday evening this Institute was re-opened for the season under new auspices. A very large membership has been secured and as most of the residents have joined, a successful future should result.

EMBEZZLEMENT AT WARRINGTON. A Sudden Temptation.

At Warrington, on Thursday, before Mr. John Fair. dough and other magistrates, William Wickenden, of Canterbury-street, was charged with embezzling 185.61 received by him on account of his employer, William Pomoll, florist, of Parigate and Warrington ltiarket- place,and also with obtaining 10s. from Miss Constance Broadbent by means of a trick. He pleaded guilty. The Chief Constable stated that prisoner had been employed' by Mr. Powell since December. An order was received from a lady at Grappenball for some cut flowers on July 39th. The flowers were supplied, and the money (IBs. 6d.) was collected by a boy, who handed it to prisoner,and he failed to account for it. Mr. Powell taxed prisoner with the offence, aid he stoutly denied it, but further inquiries having been made he admitted it and asked for leniency. With regard to-the second charge, the flowers were supplied to the order of some ladies who wished to present Miss Broadbent with a bouquet, and the account was paid. Afterwards prisoner sent a lad to Miss Broadbent to collect the money a second time, Miss Broadbent NM it, and prisoner pocketed the money. Prisoner was a native of Kent, and prior to oming to Warrington his character was everything that could be des; red. Prisoner said he ha I to meet an account and succumbed to a sudden temptation. The Chairman said the magistrates did not wish to make a gaol-bird of the prisoner, and' in view of the fact that he had been in custody for a v-eek,they sentenced him to an additional day's imprisonment, which meant that he would be released on the rising of the Court.

RANDOM READINGS.

WORDSWORTH AND COLERIDGE AS SPIES.

One of the most serio-comic incidents in our literary history is the shadowing of Wordsworth and Coleridge by a Government detective at Alforton in the spring of .1797. The two young 'poets were writing their. famous Lyrics, and were to an extent tran- scribing from Nature. Coleridge was making verbal studies of the scenes and natural ob- jects around them. Coleridge's wife and Dorothy Wordsworth completed the party. And the SorArset folk set them all down as French spies! The whole story is told by Mr. A. J. Eagleston in the Nineteenth Century. The pitch to which misunderstanding and mystification rose is' sufficiently indicated in such a passage as the following from a re- port sent up by the detective, one Walsh, to Mr. J. King, the then Permanent Under- Secretary of State for the Home DepaOment. "Christopher Triclrie and. his Wife, who live at the Dog pound at Alfoxton, told Moggs that the French people had taken-the plan of their House, mid that They had also taken the plan of all the places round that part of the Country, that a Brook runs in the front of Trickle's Housl, and the French people inquired of _Trickle wether the Brock was Navigable to the Sea, end upon being !n- -formed by Triokie that it was not. They were afterwards seen examining the Brook quite down to the Sea."

===== 0002601_19080905_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
WEED-EATING PLANT.

It appears that at last an antidote ' has been found to the noxious weeds which are so frequently the death of certain forms of plant and vegetable life in the East. Speci- mens—of this wonderful "find" • have been forwarded to the authorities at Kew Gar- dens. This plant is a blue flowering creeper botanically known as the Commelina nudi- flora linnea, but called "Rumput Greinali " by the natives of Malaysia and " Ge-war- an " by the Javanese. Although the report made. at Kew goce to show that this creeper is common throughout the Middle East, it would seem that the managers of estates and plantations have not known of its peculiarly welcome properties ` until very recently and accidentally. The prolific weed known as " lalang " is the great

===== 0002601_19080905_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
ENEMY TO RUBBER-GROWTH.

It was the accident of observing that where the blue-flowered creeper came in contact with the lale.ng the latter became much less injurious that induced a planter to send specimens to Kew. It seems that at first one begins to notice that the weeds are becom- ing less prolific where the creeper is growing among them. This improvement steadily in- creases as time goes on. and it has been found that, under the influence of this anti- dote, lalang which was formerly 4ft. or sft. in height has been reduced to one foot to !Lt. only when it etarta to flower. But, the joyful discovery having been made that here was anNndoubted setback to the weedy growth that chokes young rubber and is the .

BANE OF TILE PLANTER'S LIFE, ' the .question arose: Would the antidote itself exercise a prejudicial effect on the rubber? ' Therefore the specimens were duly submitted to Kew; and it was stated that the new creeper. is "unlikely to have any harmful effect on young' rubber-trees." Planters all over the East may therefore take heart of grace, and also take this new "medicine." In appearance, the blue-flowered Comme- line nudiflora is rather pretty, and, like the weeds which it first checks and then kills, it grows with astonishing rapidity. The amount of rubber produced annually in the Straits Settlements is, of course, very large, and the results .of the discovery and its suc- cessful 'application sre likely to be far- reaching. • A GRUESOME SUPERSTITION.

Whether•or no the custom of fighting fires on St. John's Bye was ever a custom in these parts, says the Manchester Guardian, an associated superstition does seem to have prevailed at one time. In his "Passages" Sam Bamford gives an account of the gather- ing of fern-seed (which when gathered with the correct rites—a very difficult matter— was supposed to confer invisibility) on the eve of St. John in Boggart-ho' Clough. The story told is of .three men who came at "deep fleet" to the Clough. One of the men carried a brown 'earthen dish, another a pewter platter, and the other a skull. The skull had the scalp sawn of above the eyes and left flapping like a, lid. The interior cavities were stuffed with moss and lined with clay kneaded with

BLOOD FROM HUMAN VEINS. The skull, carried by a love-sick youth, was secured to his shoulders hy three strands of unbleached flax, undyed wool, and woman's hair, from which depended a raven-black tress.- The three went forward to a stepping- place over the brook -opposite the old •Feyrin- ho', where the Boggart was supposed to live. Their object was to secure the seed of the St. John's Fern. Kneeling before the plant the dish was held underneath the fern; under that was the skull resting on the ground with the lid up. The appropriate rhymes were recited, but just at the critical moment a venerable form in a loose robe appeared; there was a great crash, and the skull cried out and glares' with its eyes. One of the men reached home - and fell unconscious; another was discovered two or three days after 'chas- ing wild birds on the Moss; whilst the one who bore the skull died within three months. Bamford got this Weird and highly pagan story from a fellow-prisoner in the prison of Coldbath Fields.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
THE "GLORY SONG."

It has • been said that no Gospel song in *history ever attained the international popu- larity of "The Glory Song" in so short a time, if at all, says Mr. Charles Gabriel, the author of "The Glory Song,", in the Quiver. ,It was written in 1900, and in less than five years it was sung around the world. The wonder of this, however, is not due to ..the eong alone, but to circumstances which took control of it. To me the most remarkable thing about it is that not one syllable of the words or one note of the music was found to need a change, and it stands to-day, word for word and note for note, exactly as I sent it to the printer. It has been criticised all over the world, and

YET PEOPLE 00 ON SINGING IT, regardless of critics or criticism. The em- bryo of my most successful songs has come to me without my seeking, yet to say they were purely inspiration would be foolish, as a great deal of thought and labour was re- quired to polish and complete them. I dis- tinctlY remember—the research and patience it required to get each stanza of "The Glory Song" to end ptoperly and intelli- gently as it does with 'That will be glory for me.' It looks simple. But take an idea, restrict yourself to so many syllables to a line of words, make three of a. four-line stanza rhyme togetheis with the fourth line common to each stanza, and a systematic ac- cent pervading the entire poem, and before your,' are finiihed your scope of vision will have been somewhat enlarged.

THE "LONG AND SHORT" OF WILLS. The will of the. late Duke of Devonshire, which contained nearly 18,000 words, is cer- tainly entitled to rank high among long- winded testaments. Probably the shortest will on record was that of a Streatham gentleman, proved a few years ago, which consisted of the words, "All for mother, C. T." The lakt will and testament of a Colonel F. E. Rigby, who died at Chicato, was written on a scrap of paper, four inches long and three wide, which, apart from the date and signatures, contained only fifteen words; eleven words answered the purpose of a recent Brooklyn testkor; and the testa- ment of Mr. 1\ B. White,. a wealthy Chicago newspaper man, ran thus: "I give every- thing te my ,wife." Another brief will was that pencilled on a small piece of card by Mr. Borekhardt, of St. Albans, as he lay dy- ino on the Matterhorn. It was addressed tn his sister: "1 am dying on Matterhorn. I leave all I possess to you, my dear OlterA God bless you," -

POSTER PRINTING. WE PRINT THE LARGEST SHEET IN THE TOWN AND DISTRICT. EXAMINER" OFFICE, WARRINGTON

It may safely be affirmed DO PLANTS that even the scientific

REMEMBER? reader, unless he be a con- firmed specialist in a particular direction, will not be much the wiser by reason of the Address delivered by Dr. FRANCIS DARWIN to the British Association yesterday. He will, in the first place, be confronted by words which can be found only in the latest dictionaries. Dr. DARWIN speaks of " varieties arising as changes in, or additions to the later stages of ontogeny," and then proceeds to remark that "this is, briefly given, the epigenetic point of view." What is ontogeny ? It is, we pre- sume, a process of development, either in plants, in the lower animals, or in man. The epigenetic point of view, we may similarly surmise, is that of those who hold that the germ of an organism does not pre-exist in the parent, but is brought into existence by the parents' powers of procreation. It is, therefore, a point of view which is opposed to ordinary theories of evolution, the defence of which Dr. DARWIN may reasonably regard as an hereditary obligation. Darwinism, as the President of the British Association admits, fs passing through a crisis. It is being assailed from now -lines of thought and observation. There is doubt where there was previous certainty of mind on the subject. Even ALFRED RUSSEL WALLACE has been accused of back- sliding. But Dr. DARWIN holds as strongly as ever to the importance of natural selection. He is convinced, he says, that the inherent strength of the doctrine will ensure its final victory over the present anti-Darwinian stream of criticism. He therefore proceeds to affirm, or rather to suggest, that the Darwinian principle can be supported from plant life, and that plants are organisms, his definition of an organism being " a machine which can be set going by touching a spring or trigger of some kind ; a machine in which energy can be set free by some kiud of releasing mechanism." We shall, we believe, be stating Dr. DAtiwises hypothesis correctly if we say that, according to his contention, plants have memory and acquire habits. He has taken up one of the latest of the investigations of his illustrious father, who was struck by the resemblance .between certain move- ments of plants and many of the actions performed unconsciously by the lower animals. His special investigations, he thinks, make the positive value of natural selection more obvious. The Address is really an argument against Weismannism, WEISMANN contending that acquired characteristics are not inherited, and, therefore, striking at the very root of the Darwinian theory. Thera must be war to the knife between the theories of WEIssuNN and that of the somatists, says Dr. DARWINN. Here,again, we pause to inquire who and what are the somatists ? They are those who believe in the inheritance of acquired characters. The designa- tion, which is new, is rather far-fetched, but has a certain applicability, soma being the Juice of a plant offered in libation to a Hindu god to strengthen him in his fight with the demons. It is identified—and here its applicability comes in— with the invigorating and immortalising principle in nature. Somatic inheritance, Dr. DARWIN declares, ties at the root of all evolution which is a proposition that he might have defended much more interestingly than he actually does. His subject had led us to expect something rather fascinating. We are most of us willing to suspect the existence of a more or less crude intelligence in certain forms of plant life. Many creeping plants, for example,suggest some sort of deliberate action. It would have been delightful if Dr. DARWIN had collected and displayed everything that seems like proof; instead of which he spoke to scientific men only in terms of science, and, we regret to say, without any fascination whatever. The address is, however, distinguished by its modesty. The PRESIDENT of the British Associa• tion avoids one argumentative temptation after another, pleading that he must speak only on the subject of his own researches. He makes no positive affirmations. Everything is tentative in his argument. He has been accused, he says, of sitting on the fence, and, as it seems to us, not without reason. On the basis of his investigations he might well have been more positive. His main contention is that ontogenetic growth is a result directly traceable to a remembering faculty. For instance, certain plants that sleep at night—those' which close their flowers or contract their leaves when daylight disappears—will continue the same movements if they are placed in a dark cellar. Here we have, he thinks, a habit built up by the alternation of day and night, a contention which is open, from the WRialtaNN point of view, to the reply that a dark cellar does not correspond to actual darkness. A man who closes his eyes when the sun is shining will still feel the daylight, and if he walks in the night, and again closes his eyes, he will similarly feel the dark ; from which we might conclude that the opening of a plant in the morning, though it was in a dark place, is not habit, but stimulus. Even Dr. DARWIN does not venture to go further than the state- ment that we have in plants som sthing which has the general character of habit. He shrinks from saying that plants have consciousness. They may have it or they may not —we cannot tell ; but "it is consistent with the doctrine of continuity," he says, "that in all living things there is something psychic, and if we accept

this point of view we must believe that in plant there exists a faint copy of what we know as consciousness in ourselves.? It is an interesting and even agreeable speculation ; but nothing more. The desire of the PRESIDENT of the British Association is to lead us up to the conclusion that natural selections attains in a blind, mechanical way such results as might, for instance, be pro- duced by a skilful gardener—that training is continually going on under an automatic master, " with endless patience and all time at his dis- posal." If the hypothesis could be sustained, all doubts about Darwinism must, of course, disappear.

Congratulations to " P. "P. AND K.'s " K., Ltd." The well•known PROGREiS. firtll of ironworks and

colliery proprietors who are thus familiarly described locally, have had a most successful year's trading notwithstanding the general slump in business all over the country. They have just announced a dividend which makes Ili per cent. for the year against 10 per cent, for the previous year, written oil the year's capital expenditure out of profits, added £20,000 to the reserve fund and carried forward £12,038. Prudent management, combined with enterprise which never lacked boldness, have made Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, Ltd., one of the soundest industrial concerns in the country. The distinctly moderate fluctuation of the share-value after the Maypole Colliery disaster was a testimony to the solidity of the company. We hope the prosperity will continue,for busy times at thillarn and Bawsey have great influence on the state of employment in Warrington. There is much anxiety felt by workingmen with regard to the winter prospects, but we sincerely trust that their fears will not be realized.

One of the best ways of BEAUTIFUL making Warrington beauti- WARRINGTON ful is to promote the culti- vation of fhwers, and there is no denying the fact that the Beautiful War. , rington Society is doing a good work in this direction. If proof were needed, it was forth- coming on Saturday when the third annual show quite eclipsed its predecessors both in size and in beauty. People no longer scoff or poke fun at the idea of Warrington having a good fl )wer show, for , it is quite evident now that beautiful blooms can be grown in Warrington, notwithstanding the menace of the smoke fiend. But perhaps the most important feature of the show is that which cannot be placed alongside the other exhibits. Prizes are offered for the most attractive gardens and it was pleasing to hear from the judges that some of those submitted for competition are models of what back gardens ought to be. Still there is room for much greater enthusiasm. Only about half of last year's exhibi- tors again had the courage to compete and more than half of these had not kept up to their previous standard. Of course, there were many new competitors but the diminution of effort on the part of the others is sincerely to be regretted. The great hope of the society is the rising generation and the work amongst the school children gives every cause for encouragement. Greater care is bestowed by the juveniles upon the plants entrusted to them, and the " Children's Corner " of Saturday's show was about the most pleasing of all the classes. The teachers are to be commended for their valuable help in training the young idea in the right way, and we hope they will continue to persevere in this branch of their labours.

The brewers' demonstration THE BREWERS' in Hyde Park on the 27th DEMONSTRATION. inst. against the Licensing Bill will be, to a large extent, an artificial one. It has bean organised by the brewing interest, and special trains will be run from several of the great towns in the Midlands, and notably from Burton-on-Trent, in order to bring thousands of demonstators to the metropolis. This marks the unreality of the whole business. There was no difficulty in obtaining ten of thousands of people in London itself to come to Hyde Park in support of the Licensing Bill, but the materials for a counter-demonstration cannot be found in the metropolis, and hence the importation of a great number of provincial beer drinkers. 'When fares are to be wholly or partially paid, it is always easy to get people to London to demonstrate in support of almost any cause whatever. The sup- porters of the Licensing Bill could give the demonstrators against it a good deal of trouble. They might, for one thing, gather in the park in great numbers before the brewers' demonstrators arrived, but the general disposition in the temper- ance party is to leave the gathering alone. Nothing in the shape of a rival meeting will be attempted ;but in all likelihood, a great deal of literature bearing on the Bill, and on the cause of temperance, will be distributed among the pro. cessionists as they march along.

On Friday a hundred years A ME3I3RIBLE ago yesterday the extra. ANNIVERSARY, ordinary news was being carried over England of the total defeat of the French Army in Portugal, and its offer of capitulation. The naval power of France and Spain had been broken at Trafalgar three years before. The French had entered Spain in 1807,and Joseph Bonaparte was acclaimed King 'of Spain at Madrid on July 12th, to retire in a fortnight. The Battle of Vimiara occurred on August 21st, but the news took a fortnight to reach these shores, and when it came the popular enthusiasm knew no bounds, just as, in another fortnight, indignation and disgust found the most extreme expression when news o tha Convention of Cintra shewed that, after all, Junot had not been followed and pressed to uncon- ditional surrender. The public required a scape- goat, and Wellesley was pitched on. The "Times" had anticipated even "the anal dissolution of the Continental Tyranny and the overthrow of the Tyrant,' and the man who was to be Bonaparte's match became the butt of scorn and criticism. His name alone had appeared on the armistice which preceded the disappointing Convention. He had, however, acted under protest at the require- ment of Sir Hew Dalrymple, and when a Court of Inquiry came to look into the position the proceed- ings took the form of a sustained indictment by Wellesley of both his superiors in command. Four months later Wellesley was once more in command 'of the British forces in the Peninsula, and there followed that succession of brilliant victories which 'included Oporto and Talavera before six months had passed. Cuidad Rodrigo, Badaojz,Salamanca land the crowning triumph of Vittoria came in 1812 Ithe year of the retreat from Moscow, and Wellington made his way into France in the following year. The "Times" quotations just now, from its files of a hundred years ago, give a graphic and curious insight into the state of public feeling in those trying time's.

The latest issue of the 310 HE PSYCHICAL Proceedings of the Society RESEARCH for Psychical R9search WoNDEns. # contains a singularly interesting report. It is the sort of report which has been long awaited. Mr. F. W. H. Myers, the author of "Human Personality," left it on record that he desired after death to make communication with survivors by means of cross-correspondence which would show that the communication was neither telepathic nor springing fcom the activities of the medium's subliminal consciousness. These communications have arrived. A "Mrs. Holland," in India, has received incomplete messages by automatic handwriting which have their complement apparently in similar writings by Mrs. Verrall. In Mrs. Verrall's case comments were made on the entire correspondence, of which Mrs. Verrall received only part. The significance of some of Mrs. Holland's writings she did not perceive until someone pointed it out. One message began and ended with an initial, and at the end of each of three paragraphs was part of a data " 17/1/01," which had been split into three. The initials were those of Mr. Myers's name, and the date January 17th, 1901, was the date of Mr. Myers's death mentioned in "Human Per-

„

===== 0002601_19080905_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
sonality." A cryptic message purported to come from Dr. Richard Hodgson, and when someone else had discovered the partly-indicated key Mrs. Holland saw that an inconsequent array of letters and figures gave D. Hodgson's name in two forms. Mr. Piddington, who has examined Dr. Hodgson's papers, says the cryptogram is " extremely characteristic," and says it in italics. These are merely indications of the nature of some of the script, and they are claimed to illustrate Mr. Balfour's dictum to the Society that there is " strong ground for supposing that outside the world, as we have, from the point of science, been in the habit of conceiving it, there does lie region . • . with regard to which some experimental information may be laboriously gleaned."

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

We may as well place on record this week another of those brief and bright sayings that crystallise the proper relations of man and wife. A female witness at Brentford declared the other day that a gentleman in the case "was using language to me that even my husband does not use. Since the death of him who was "more like a friend than a husband," there has been no such intimate searchlight upon our social conditions.

* * * The German who finds himself paying about 20 per cent, more for everything he buys now that the tariff is in full force can at least console himself in his quiet evenings by reading the Tariff Reform literature that shows that all his taxes are in reality paid by the foreigner. That is just the argument that his cold Teutonic mind will have no difficulty in grasping. * * *

To bet or not to bet ?—will be a serious question for the members of the speculative fraternity wha carry on operations in the public streets. The police activity and the heavy penalties imposed nearly every week on some delinquent or other, have given pause to some of the bookmakers. Personally, we hope the police will persevere in their crusade against what is certainly the worst class of betting, inasmuch as it lightens the pockets of those who are already poor and needy, and provides an easy living to many who have a distaste for real hard work.

* * * The Borough Member and his wife will soon be back in London for the re-opening of Parliament. Al. present they are enjoying a holiday at one of Mr. Crosfield's favourite resorts —St. Moritz in the Engadine. Here there is a fashionable and cosmo- politan gathering and out-door sports of the most attractive kind. Mrs. Crosfield has just lately been demonstrating her skill at lawn-tennis, and while she was beaten in the final for the tournament at the Kulm, she and her sister; Miss Elliadi, won the doubles with ease.

* * * Mr. Arthur Henderson, the chairman of the Labour Party; spoke out strongly in favour of Free Trade at a miners meeting in Monmouthshire on Monday : The Labour Party and the wage-earners of the country generally were doing the best possible service to the community in fighting for the reten- tion of the present system. This did not solve the unemployment question, but he would emphati- cally make the assertion, if he were out of emplor ment, that he would prefer to ba- so in a country whet() Free Trade was in operation rather than in a country where the prices of provisions and commodities generally were much higher. That puts the case very well —Tariff Reform as a remedy for unemployment is only a quack remedy. * * * Councillor Wilkinson has ,g touched the spot," or found it, to be more strictly accurate. When the members of the Council assembled on the river bank at Howley last week to select a site for the new foot- bridge they were unable to agree. But Councillor Wilkinson stayed behind and found a site which be is convinced will cost the Corporation nothing and will facilitate the erection of the bridge. Apparently it will be necessary for him to take the Council down to the river again and either convince them or push them in. On Tuesday they were unable to adopt a tender for construction, and matters remain as they were. We hope the Howley Bridge will not earn the reputation for delay which belongs ta its hoary fore- father —Warrington Bridge! * * * Yesterday's fire at Messrs. Rylands' works revealed the adequateness of Warrington's resources for fire- fighting. Within a few minutes of the alarm the two borough engines were on the spat, while those of Messrs. Crosfields and the Wilderspool Brewery arrived very soon after. For a time they turned out more smoke than the burning warehouse, but their united efforts soon got the fire under and prevented what might have been a disastrous outbreak. only the day before, the insurance people had surveyed the, whole works and remarked that with Messrs. Rylands precautions they considered an outbreak of fire almost impossible. The fire-fiend's reply has been prompt and convincing.* * * Miss Broadbent has written to the Health aim' mittee complaining about the emission of black smoke. The matter was referred to at the Council meeting ee Tuesday by Alderman Bennett, the champion smoke" fighter, who expressed the hope that Miss Broadbent 's letter would not be overlooked. Councillor Evans (chairman of the Health Committee) replied that the Committee were dealing with the question on what "they deemed to be fair lines without unduly hamper- ing the traders of the town." This is the stock replY to questions about the smoke nuisance, and we should like to say here that until the Committee alters its tactics and begins to "hamper the traders" no pre- gress will be made towards lessening the evil.

* * The eisteddfod which has been held this week 0 Llangollen must not be confused with other eistedd; fodau held during the year. The provincial ann county eisteddfodau are many, the parochial ones are still more numerous .„ in fact, there is hardly a town" ship in Wales that does not hold an eisteddfod of son° size during the year. But there is only ene "National " Eisteddfod a year, and it is held North and South Wales alternately, with occasione! excursions to London, Liverpool, and Chester. lb cannot be held anywhere or by anybody. The idea ie that the whole nation is holding it, and the Executive Committee, which has had to work very hard for nearly two years at Llangollen, for instance, is merelY acting for the nation. Proper appeal—and humble' must be made two years in advance by the townsmen who desire to set up the house of the Eisteddfod for e brief week among them, the joint arbiters being the Gorsedd. or Senate of the Bards and the National Eisteddfod Association. One of the conditions is the furnishing of a guarantee of a thousand pounds, unless a township is prepared to do the thing royall it has no chance in these days of huge prizes.

This is a moot t of transitions, and lower temperatures must ~ expected, though frosts, even towards the close of the month, are rare. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

THE SALVATION ARMY AT WIDNES. FOUNDATION-STONE LAYING. Directly opposite the new Liberal Club premises in Widnes-road, there was a pleasant little ceremony on :Monday afternoon, when four foundation-stones were laid upon the site obtained by the Salvation Army authorities for their new hall. The ceremonies were Performed by Mrs. J. Wilkinson, of Timperley, Alderman W. W. Gossage ; Colonel R. Wilson, Provisional commander, for Mr. T. Sutton Limmis; and Mr. S. Kershaw, for Mr. J. Lewis,.„ a former treasurer to the local Army Corps.— Ws Worship the Mayor presided, and after the presenta- tion of a bouquet to Mrs. Owens (Mayoress), the chairman said he was sorry for the inclement weather, but they had to face worse difficulties than that. It Pleased him greatly to be present that day, because they had met on other occasions, when they felt that „....e'rcrything was not going on as well as desirable. However, they all knew the Salvation Army, and it was not altogether new to them. He was glad of it because they always had a need for it. They had made very worthy effects to better the conditions of life of the Working classes, and their would be always room for their work. The building in course of erection is the large hall, 3.lft. by 80ft., which will cost £7OO. After the completion of this a start will be made on the Children's hall, which will cost £3OO. 'Of the £l,OOO necessary to pay for the whole Mr. T. Sutton Timmis subscribed £5OO. Upwards of £9lO has already been collected. FIREMAN'S MYSTERIOUS DROWNING. At Widnes Accident Hospital on Monday evening. ' S. Brighouse, coroner, held an inquest on James --Nrurphy, aged thirty-five years, of 207, Grafton-street, Liverpool, whose body was picked up near Oglet, Speke, on Saturday afternoon.—Cecilia Murphy, said deceased Was her husband, and she last saw him alive at half-past six on the 22nd ult at home. He was a fireman on board the Mauretania, and was to have sailed for New York. He was sober at the time and went to catch the half-past seven tender (Skirmisher) to take him on board. The Coroner \read an extract from the ship's log which had been sent from Queenstown on Saturday. "At half-past on the 22nd, James Murphy, thirty-six years age, committed suicide by jumping overboard. Every effort was made to save him, but failed."—The Coroner: Was he content in the ship ?—He said he would have Signed on in the White Star Line if he could have got a ship, because the money was not sufficient for him. Four slays before he arrived he was hurt in the stomach With a" slicer," and had been under the ship's doctor. R. Burkehill, a sailor on the Skirmisher, said he heard the alarm raised from aboard the Mauretania. Every effort was made to get to him, ani a man named Peter O'Hara made a gallant attempt at the rescue but fciled.—Captain Summerfleld, of the Zillah Steamship Company, said be picked the body up near Spoke, and brought it into Widnes.—The Coroner said they had only the log to rely upon for evidence, but it would not do for them to commit themselves on that state- Inent alone. It would be unfair, without any direct evidence, so say that he jumped overboard.—The jury returned a verdict of " Found drowned." - RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. ...The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Lxuardians was held at the Dutton Workhouse on Tuesday, Mr. Joseph Spruce presiding. THE DUST NUIiANCE. The County Council, referring to the dust nuisance caused by motor cars intimated that they were prepared to treat the main trunk roads with calcium chloride or other dust laying material if the local Council would bear two-thirds of the cost. The letter was referred to the Highways Committee.

HOSPITAL ACCOMZIODATION. At a meeting of the Isolation Hospital Committee it was reported that a letter had been received from the Local Government Board drawing attention to the report for 1907 of the Medical Officer of Health with respect to hospital accommodation and asking whether the Council were considering the question of increasing the accommodation. The committee passed a resolution to the effect that as the number of oases of infectious disease in the Council's district had recently decreased to a great extent, they hoped there would be no neces- sity for increased accommodation for a lengthened period. This resolution was confirmed. lIRALTH OF THE DISTRICT. Dr. Adams reported the frillowing cases of scarlet fever :—One at Frodsham Lordship, three at Weston, and one at Halton. Kingsley schools had been closed for a further fortnight in ensequence of an extensive outbreak of whooping cough. Over 65 children were known to be affected by the disease.

EARLE STOWN AND NEWTON.

NEWTON A.F.C.

- - The annual meeting of the Newton Football Club was held last week at the Pied Bull Hotel. In review- ing the season's work the secretary, Mr. Davidson, in his report, said that the Committee did everything in their power last season to get a strong 'team together (even wranging a friendly game with Chester instead of a further trial match), and the result of the first nine league games went to show that their efforts bad not been unsuccessful, In five of them they were beaten by the odd goal. One of the players had always been featly, encouraging others, and never losing heart, always doing his utmost for the village organisation, and always ready to play in any position without demur. This was J. Lucas, who along with Saxon went right through the season with them. The financial statement showed that the total receipts were £147, as against £167 the previous saason. The gate receipts showed a falling off, which was attributed to the fact that Earlestown and Ashton were no longer in the same league as them. On the expenditure side—league expenses and referees% fees were heavy, but they saved on the wages bill, and on the whole thought their deficit compared favourably with previous seasons.—The following officers were elected for the coming season :—President, Mr. Hargreaves; hon. secretary, Mr. Davidson; bon. treasurer, Mr. P. McHardy • committee, Messrs. B. Herds, F. Hargreaves. H. 'Pearson_, C. Middleton ; captain, J. Lucas; vice-captain, EL Fletcher. The following players have been registered : Smith, Griffith; Sergeant, Watkinson, Evans, Lucas, Gardner, Bowker, Holden, Johnson, Fletcher and Smart. 'NEWTON PETTY SESSIONS. At Newton, on Thursday last week before Messrs. Wood, Herron and Collingwood. A NEIGHBOURS' Qtriann.Catherine Fahey was summoned for assaulting Agnes M. Ryan, on July 14th, at Houghton-street, Earlestown.—Plaintiff said defend- ant threw a bucketful of such foul stuff over her that it made her ill for two days.—Defendant said she did it because plaintiff had been scandalizing her.—Plaintiff denied this, and the Bench bound defendant over to keep the peace for three months. POLES AT VARIANCE.—John Baratinsky was charged with assaulting and wounding William Miller, in the Ram's Head Vaults, Earlestown, on August 22nd, by hitting him with a glass and cutting his head.—Mrs. Zelansky was sworn to trfferpret the evidence, the parties being Lithuanian Poles working at Haydock Collieres.-:-Plaintiff said he was wounded on the head by the defendant, during a quarrel.—P.C. Lennie said he was called in to stop the disturbance. The parties were taken to the police station, where a wound two inches long, and down to the bone, was found on plaintiff's head. Defendant made a shift to tell the Bench in indifferent English, that the other man hit him first, and he was set upon by " five together on me one, before I hit him with glass I "—Fined 10s. and costs.

ST. HELENS BRASS BAND CEIAMPIO'NSHIP. The St. Helens and District Amateur Brass Band Association heldits fifth annual championship contest on Saturday at Parr, when seven bands entered for the two contests. There was a good attendance. Mr. John Gladney, of Manchester, was the adjudicator. In the march contest the selection being open to each band to choose, Irlam St. John's took first place, the con- ductor being Mr. Jennings, Sutton-road. St. Helens (conductor Mr. Greenwood) was second. The chief event was the selection contest. "Ii Trovatore," a lengthy and difficult piece, was the test. The winning band was the 4th West Lancashire R.F.A. Band, of Liverpool, and the judge said that how the band managed to get through tho selection so credit- ably would alseays be a marvel to him, but they were undoubtedly the best. Mr. Greenwood was the con- ductor of this band, as well as of the three bands next in order of merit. Parr Temperance was second,Raven- head 3rd, Sutton-road 4th, Irlam St. John's sth, Nut- grove (Mr. Hill) 6th, and Moss Bank (Mr. Appleton) 7th. Parr Temperance being the first of the local bands., were awarded the challenge cup offered by the association, and, having won the cup twice before, it now becomes the property of the band.—Mr. T. C. Davies (president) handed the cup to Mr. Lancaster, of Parr Temperance, and said that,although there had been a fairly good attendance, he did not consider that the competition was as well patronised by the public as it ought to have been.

PENKETH.

FAREWELL SnitmoN.—On Sunday evening the Rev. E. J. Gawne preached his farewell sermon in the Wesleyan Chapel, there being a large congregation. The Ray. E. J. Gawne has made a host of friends in Penketh during the two years be has been minister there,and he goes with the best wishes of all. The text of his sermon was taken from the first Epistle of Paul to the Roman's the first part and 12th verso. The sermon was of a most edifying character and was keenly listened to with close attention. A BICYCLE STOLEN.—An impudent theft was perpe- trated at Great Sankey the other evening. A gentleman who had cycled from Warrington to make a call on Mr. Fred Johnson, Bank Villas, left his bicycle in the walk of the garden, and when he was about to leave shortly afterwards he found that the machine had been stolen. The gentleman at once informed the police of the theft, but up to the present nothing has been heard of the machine.

The best of all methods of progression in. Central Africa—however astonishing it may seem—is, accord- ing to Mr. Winston Churchill, in. the "Strand Magazine," the bicycle : In the dry season, the paths through the bush, smoothed by the feet of natives, afford an excellent surface. Even when the track is only two feet wide, and when the densest jungle rises on either side, and almost meets above the head, theloicyclo skims along, swishing through the grass and brush- ing the encroaching bushes, at a fine pace ; and, although at every few hundred yards sharp rocks, loose stones, a water-course, or a steep hill compel dismounting, a good seven miles an hour can usually be maintained. And think what this means. From my own experience I should suppose that with a bicycle twenty-five to thirty miles a day could regularly be covered in Uganda, and if only the porters could keep up, all journeys could be nearly trebled, and every white officer's radius of action proportionately increased. Nearly all the British officers ••Mr. Churchill met already possessed and used bicycles, and even the native chiefs are beginning to acquire them. The motor, we suppoao, is sure to follow—and after it the dust nuisance !
