WATER POLO.

WARRINGTON v. ST. HELENS.

These teams, met at the Warrington Corporation Baths on Thursday evening in a Liverpool and District League match. In the first half Warrington defended the deep end, and the visitors were soon attacking, Gibbons opening their score. Warrington played up more spiritedly, and Allen, receiving from Sutton, sent in a shot which went too high. St. Helens again attacked, and Gibbons placed a second goal to their credit, which he speedily followed with a third. Walker then opened the score for Warrington with a splendid shot from half may. St. Helens again assumed the aggressive, and A. Barr registered another goal. Ilalf- time arrived with !It. Helens leading by 4 goals to 1. After the interval there was some exciting play. W. Ashton swam three parts the lengths of the bath, but his final shot failed. He was warmly cheered for a splendid effort. The St.Helens goal was hotly assailed, and eventually W. Ashton scored. IVarrington kept up the attack, and Sutton added another goal. Result : St. Helens 4 goals, Warrington 3 goals. A fifty yards handicap for elementary school boys ' resulted as follows :-1, 13. Harding ; 2, S. Watkins 3, S. Cooper. '

The Examiner Saturday, September 5, 1908.

" Brotherhood" Meetings for Men. The opening meeting of the " Brotherhood " formed , in connection with Golborne-street Baptist Church will held to-morrow afternoon at 3.0 p.m. when the President (the Rev. R. E. Letheren) will give an address. A special feature of "The Brotherhood" will he the conversational discussion after each address and the encouragement of the members to take part in the meetings. Owing to lack of accommoda- tion, pending the erection of new premises, a room has been temporarily hired at over No. 5, Regent- street, corner of Legh-street. The room will be available for use on week nights and though at, present the members are bard at work preparing for ' new buildings it is proposed to form at least the nucleus of an Institute. By and by it is hoped to , have large rooms set apart for men and women where ! pleasant evenings can be spent and societies formed for mutual improvement and helpful service. All men are heartily welcome. Orchard Robbers Fined.

At Warrington yesterday, three youths named George Isherwood, of New-road; Oswald Cook, of New-road ; and William Gamble, of Knutsford-road, were charged with stealing a quantity of apples and pears, the property of Mrs. Broadbent, The Hollies, Latchford. Inspector Williamson stated that during the past.few weeks there had been a series of robberies from Mrs. Broadbent's orchard and this week con- siderable damage had been done to valuable trees.l Early that morning Constables Monks and Middleton secreted themselves and saw the defendants enter the orchard and commenced picking fruit off the trees. They gave chase and caught Gamble and the other defendants were arrested at 'their work. Isherwood admitted that on Thursday morning he had five apples, Cook said he had nine and Gamble fourteen.— The Mayor said they were determined to put a stop to that kind of thing and defendants wonld be fined l Os. each.

_ The attendance at the meeting of this society on Sunday was good. The Secretary (Ur. B. Farrar) presided, and Mr. James Shaw gave the address upon A Sure Foundation, or, Wise Building." The con- tention was that a good moral life was good, but the greater need was a life influenced by the life of Christ. Mr. C. Cotton read the lesson, and the Rev. J. Yonge offered prayer. Miss Armstrong sang" I need Thee every hour" and "Giver of Life" in a pleasing manner. The .chairman, in inviting the brethren to join in the memorial service to the late Mr. Sankey to be held in the evening, said such a service had a claim upon them, as they had for fifteen years so richly enjoyed Sankey's hymnal in their services. Mr. Payton presided at the organ.

Local Athletic Successes.

At the Catholic Young Men's Society's Sports at Liverpool on Saturday, Y. R. Moorfield, Warrington, off the nine yards mark secured the second prize in the 160 yards open race, and the same athlete won the 220 yards race, from the 161 yards mark. In the mile flat race at the same sports, G. Watmough, Warring- ton (86) ran into third place. The Warrington athletes were very innch in evidence at the Lincoln sports also held on Saturday. In the half-mile handi- cap, A. Siddall, Warrington A.O, with an allowance of 60 yards, won by six yards in lmin. 55secs. Following on this success he ran third in the one mile handicap. In this race he had a start of 140 yards. In the one mile walking handicap, J. George, with 210 yards and W. H. Price, with 250 yards, were third and fourth respectively. In the furlong race, E. Lightfoot, 18 yards start, was beaten by four yards for first place, while in a sprint of 120 yards the same athlete gained the fourth prize.

TENNIS, CROQUET AND GARDEN REQUISITES.— J. S. GARLAND Ironmonger, Bridge street, Warrington.—Advt: The Late Mr. H. N. Houghton. _

St. James' "Parish Magazine.'" says :—" On Satur- day, August 22nd, at Stockton Heath, there was laid to rest all that was mortal of Hamlet Newell Houghton, wko for many years was well-known and greatly respected in this town and neighbourhood. Mr. Floughton had long been associated with St. James' Church He had been sidesman and churchwarden and at the time of his death he was a member of the Church Council, a lay representative and a manager of the day school. In a variety of other ways he served the Church, cheerfully giving his help in many and different directions. Mr. Houghton was a Church- man of strong and decided opinions, and was entirely sincere and conscientious. Religion to him was the. thing of first importance. He loved the Church, and had a strong realization of the grace of the sacraments, and so every Sunday and Holy Day bond him at the Eucharist a faithful and most devoted ' reverent worshipper. Mr. Houghton had suffered from a long and painful illness sad for nearly a year had been completely invalided. The end came on August 19th, when he passed quietly away. 'May he rest in peace." PUBLIC BENEFIT.—UNDER rosa CLOCK FON BNST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. Warrington Band of Hope Union. A special meeting of the Council and workers of the Warrington Band of Hope Union was held at Gregory's Cafe on Tuesday evening presided over by Mrs. James Dodds, President of the Union. Mr. W. Chandos Wilson attended as a deputation from the Lancashire and Cheshire Band of Hope Union to explain in detail the "Forward Movement " about to be inaugurated by the Counties Union. Mr. Wilson in a vigorous and enthusiastic address set forth very plainly the principal features of the movement which comprise: (1) a great effort to double the-membership of Bands of Hope and Juvenile Temperance Societies to be accomplished by house to house canvass, laying the claims of total abstinence Wore the inhabitants, the object being to reach the many thousand children who are at present outside the societies,to pledge them against strong drink and so prevent them becoming victims of intemperance. (2) A great financial effort to raise a sum of money to ensure the continuance of temperance teaching in day schools and liquidate other liabilities of the Counties Union. The whole project is to be fully lanitched at a great gathering of i representatives of every society n the two counties, which number over 2,000, to be held in the Free Trade Hall, Manchester, on Saturday, October 10th, 1908. At the close Of Mr. Wilson's address, the president (Mrs. Dodds) xpressed her approval of the scheme and emphasised the necessity of endeavouring to prevent the rising generation from drifting into the evil habits of intemperance. A resolution pledging the Warrington Band of Hope Union to do all in its power to help on the" Forward Movement" was pro- posed by Mr. Muir, seconded by Mr. Dale and supported by Rev. J. S. Hughes, Mr. W. Wright, Mr, Hamblett and Mr. Jones, after which a hearty vote of thanks was accorded Mr. Wilson for his address, and Mrs. Dodds for presiding. The singing of the Doxology brought a very enthusiastic gathering to a close.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" Charley's Aunt."

Everybody has heard of " Chatley's Aunt from Brazil, where the nuts come from.' Most people have seen her, and the majority are \not averse to see. hag her again. ,We don't know how long Brandon Thomas's side-splitting farce has been running, but it does not seem to decrease one iota in popularity. Personally, we had not seen the 'estimable aunt for about a dozen years until Monday evening, when we found her at the Royal Court Theatre looking as hearty as ever and "still running," with the infatuated spettigue in full chase behind. We hardly need recount her adventures here, they are surely sufficiently well-known by now. The company, which is from the Royalty Theatre, London, is an exception- ally strong one and gives an exhilarating present- ment of the' farce. Mr. G. A. Seeger plays the dual roles of Lord Fancourt Babberley and " Charley's Aunt" as if they had become second nature to him. As the lady from Brazil, Donna Lucia d'Alvadorez, he not only makes up capitally, but dis- plays a sprightliness and comicality suggestive of W. S. Penley himself. The audience roared again and again with laughter at the predicaments Charley's Aunt finds " herself " in at the result of the conduct of those two reckless "undergrads," Jack Chesney and Charley Wykeham (Mr. Hastings Lynn and Mr. Basil Lynton). These two both infuse the necessary amount of "go" into their parts and nothing lags while they are on the stage. The two girls who are the innocent cause of all the mischief and mirth, Kitty Verdun and Amy Spettigne,' are represented by Miss Olivia Glynn and Miss Grace Russell. Their parts are not particularly exacting, but they are inter- preted with a natural charm and grace which contrast pleasantly with the boisterous fun going on all around. Mr. Roy Byford gives an exception 11 y 'clever study of the love-sick Spettigue in his chase after the elusive Donna Lucia and her millions. Equally good is Mr. McCausland Stewart, as Colonel Sir Francis Chesney, Bart., the retired Indian officer who enters for the same prize, but retires after the first heat and finds an old sweetheart in the real Donna Lucia (Hiss Vera Gerald) when that striking lady eventually turns up. Miss Gerald and Miss Evelyn Gray (as Ela Delahay) both acquit themselves with credit. The part of Brasset, the solemn visaged college scout, is played with rare skill by Mr. Sidney Compton. The farce is handsomely staged and dressed,and appeals to all with the freshness of a first season.

There 18 a curtain raiser each evening entitled "The Lancashire Sailor," also by Brandon Thomas, but we cannot compliment the author upon his incursion into the realm of cheap sentiment. " The Lancashire Sailor" seems incongruous all through, and Jr. Hastings Lynn, in the ti/le role, has difficul- ties with the Lancashire dialect which one can sympathise with. The curtain-raiser has the single merit of malting one feel sufficiently miserable to welcome " Charley's Aunt" with a glad heart.

NATIONAL COUNCIL OF C.E. TEACHERS. Warrington District Branch.

A meeting in connection with the above Society was held on Tuesday in Heath Side School, when the mem- bers present were told how the society was progressing throughout the country, together with its aims and aspirations. Mr. STANSFIELD the president af the locial branch, in introducing Mr. Scott, who gave an address, said , that as our representative on the-National Council, he had done splendid work in every way, and that as organising secretary for the whole Council, Mr. Scott had probably done much more work than he was physi- cally fit for hut that it must be very gratifying to him to know that the society is no or on a thoroughly good business footing; that new branches were forming everywhere ; that the Executive of the National Union of Teachers has shown that it has been influenced by its formation in its treatment of Church School questions, while the N.U.T. has acknowledged that the new body is not in opposition to itself, by the two sets of officials acting in conjunction in many ways. Also it was his (Mr. Stanstleld's) opinion, that some reason for the dropping of the last Eduo ition Bill might probably be found in the awakening of the Church Teachers in thus banding together to protect their , Interests: which were admittedly often diverse from those of teachers generally. , Mr. SCOTT in his address said that there were 65 branches of the Church Council in existence, comprising a membership of over 4,000, without including branches joining lately, and others on the point of formation. fie wished most strongly to emphasise the fact that the i Church Council was not n opposition to the National Union of Teachers as evidenced by the fact that the N.U.T. offices are lent for the transaction of Church Council business, and that the N.U.T. officials are accepting and giving help to the elucida- tion of cases of hardship to Church teachers, which had been investigated partly or wholly by the Church Council's officials. lie defended the formation of the society by citing the fact that under the Education Act of 1902, Church Teachers whose schools were closed by any education authority could be dis- missed absolutely, while by the same Act,those teachers working in any Board or Council school, must, in the event of the school being closed, be re-instated in some position of equal value to that lost. (And this distinc- tion became more alarming still to Church teachers under the "Contracting out" clause of the late Education Bill). But the N.U.T. Executive was com- posed almost solely of Council school teachers who naturally champion their own interests to the exclusion of those teaching in "Voluntary" schools. Then at the Same time if they considered that 128 Church schools had been closed during the last five years includ- ing appointments of over 300 head teachers, many of whom were unable to obtain a situation at all equal in value to that lost, while others had to leave the profession, they must see the necessity of some Society to look after their special interests in addition to the He hoped that the Church Council need not be a permanent institutionl but that it would be, necessary until the " non-provtded " Church schools 1 were part of a National System with one class of school only. lie considered, denominational teaching to be necessary from a national point of view, but that Church school management must change. In his opinion settlement is possible, if all teachers appointments be in the hands of the Education Committee ; but that such appointments must from a religious standpoint, be in accordance with the views of the parents in each school area. In conclusion, he hoped that the members present would make their influence fell bath in the interests of the Church Council and also in strengthen- ing the hands of Church teachers as far as the N,U.T. Executive um concerned.

FIRE AT MESSRS, RYLANDS' WORKS Four Engines at Work. £2,000 DAMAGE

Considerable excitement was caused in Warrington at 3.45 yesterday afternoon by the news that a fire had broken out at the works of Messrs. lands' Bros. . Ltd., wire manufacturers, Church-street. Hundreis • of people hurried to the scene and Church-street was soon impassable. It appears that the fire broke out in the wheel race in the oldest portion of the wire i drawing department, and friction s supposed to be the cause. A number of workmen saw smoke issuing from the box which covers the wheel and on raising the lid flames belched forth. The fire spread with ' such great rapidity that three floors of the block of buildings which is sixty yards long and four storeys high were involved in a few minutes. Information was telephoned to the Fire Station and the Brigade turned out very promptly with the engines " Captain "and " Major ' under the direc- tion of Superintendent Turner. Deputy-Superintendent Turner is employed at the works and he devoted his efforts to directing the works firemen who got their own appliances to work from the steam pumps and helped to keep the fire in check until the arrival of the Borough Brigade. As the outbreak assumed serious proportions telephonic messages were sent to the other local brigades, and the Wilderspool men in charge of Superintendent Croughton and Crosfield's brigade under the direction of Engineer Whitby were shortly on the scene. The whole of the warehouses and buildings adjoining the wire drawing department were in considerable danger and it was also feared that the outbreak would extend to the engine house, in which event the whole of the works would have been stopped. The firemen, however, worked magnificently and succeeded in confining the flames to the one black of buildings. Their task was a difficult one owing to the situation of this department, which is in the centre of the work 3, and they are to be complimented on preventing a disastren't conflagration. As it was the two rooms devoted to wiredrawing were burnt out and the stock of bright wire in the warehouse was greatly damaged by water. In addition a number of wiredrawers' tools were rendered useless by the great heat. It was fortunate that the electric wires by which, the whole of the works are supplied with light and power were not damaged. Fred Clegg, a fireman in the Warrington brigade, met with an unfortunate accident. lle was manipu. ' lating a hose pipe from the roof of an adjoining build- ing when a slate fell across his left hand and severely cut the forefinger and thumb. Ire was at once taken to the Infirmary and his injury was attended to. By about five o'clock the fire was well in hand but a contingent of the Warrington brigade remained on the premises for a considerable time in case of emergency. The damage, which is covered by insur- ance, is roughly estimated at £2,003. It is probable that thirty or forty hands will temporarily ha thrown out of work.

WARRINGTON BANKRUPTCY COURT. __— Friday.—Before :qr. Registrar Ridgway. COMMERCIAL TRAVELLER'S FAlLURE.—Willianl Scott, 39, Orford-road, Warrington, commercial traveller, lately residing at 115, Red Earth-road,Darwen, and formerly carrying on business under the style or firm of William Scott and Co., at Norfolk-street works, Darwen. as a colour manufacturer, appeared for his pullic examination. The statement of affairs disclosed liabilities amounting to £94 14s. and assets totalling £lo2s. Gd., leaving a dafi;iency of £75 us. Gd. Debtor attributed his insolvency to inability to realize his mortgaged property, and pressure by the mortgagees. He accounted for his deficiency by the deprecia- tion of the premises known *as Norfolk-street works, Darwen, which he purohased in 1902 for £930 and which he now valued at £034 10s., being merely a pro forral valuation equal to the amount of the mortgage debts. The debtor stated that he commenced business as a colour manufacturer at the Norfolk-street works, Dar wen, in 1902, with £lOO free capital, being £269 in cash and £llO the value of an equity in a dwelling-house. He traded under the style of William Scott and Co. On Bth October, 1901, the debtor assigned his business to the School Brow Colour Com- pany, Ltd., of Warrington, for £l3O, payable as to £ll5 in cash, and as to £285 by the allotment to the debtor of 2S Cl fully paid ordinary shares in the com- pany. The debtor at the same time also entered into an agreement for service with the Company as general manager, and he stated that he had acted in that capacity since at a salary of £2OO a year. He first became aware of his insolvency in Fehuary 1938.—The debtor was examined at some length by Mr. Johnson, assistant official receiver. He stated that there was a judgment summons against him at the instance of the Trinity Sick Society, Darwen, for £7OO and he was ordered to pay it off at the rate of £2 a week. He had an income of £2OO as manager of the Colour Company.—ln answer to Mr. Cawley, , barrister, who represented the Trinity Sick Society, debtor admitted that he had made no effort to pay the £2 a week. He could not possibly do so. It took fully £2OO a year to keep the household going and he could not manage on any less.—The examination was adjourned until October 2nd.

Coin DEALER'S CASE.—The next case was that of John Emmess, 2, Parr-street, Warrington, coal dealer, whose statement of affairs showed liabilities amounting to £4fi3 10s. 6d. and assets of £32 Bs. 6d., leaving a deficiency of .R 431 2s. Bankrupt attributed his insolv- ency to bad trade, lack of capital, comretition, loss by death and sales of horses, interest on loans from money lenders. lie commenced business as a coal dealer and carter in October, 1993 with £23 cash capital and a horse and lurry which he had purchased previously, and he purchased the good will of the bagging round from his father for £23. Since February, bankrupt admitted that he had sold the bulk of his chattel assets, including a horse, two lurries, a cart and a piano, for an aggregate sum of £3O, of which sum £l5 was deposited to file his patition and the remainder had been spent in his business. The bankrupt also sold a governess car for £l7 10s. to a creditor who did not pay for it, but claimed to retain the amount against a contra account of RCO. Bankrupt stated that he had not kept any books of account whatever, and that he had never while in business prepared any statement of his accounts and liabilities. He first became aware of his insolvency in March, 1903, and had contracted debts since then in the I hope of trade improving.—Examined by the assistant I Official Receiver, bankrupt strongly denied that his failure was brought about by drinking habits, and conse- quent neglect of business affairs.—The examination was closed.

A WARNING TO TElt PUBLIC.

The Mayor has received the following letter from the Mayor of IVigan : The Nfayor's Parlour, Wigan,3lst August, 1903. Dear Sir,—Maypole Colliery Disaster Relief Fund. Informa- tion is continually reaching 03 of persons publicly soliciting contributions on behalf of the above in many towns. should be glad if you would ask your police to take steps to sea that such persons are authorized collectors, as we have reason to believe that the generous public are being imposed on 'by professional beggars The Relief Committee has taken steps to relieve all sufferers, including those out of employment, in consequence of the disaster, and there is no justificz- tion for individual begging. I would suggest that no person be allowed to collect in your district unless authorized by yourself on behalf of the fund.—Yours faithfully,

SA.NI WODD, Mayor. CO-OPERATIVE HELP.

PROHIBITION IN THE STATES.

Oklahozni Mixes :Drinks.

THIRSTY MEN LOOK ON TEARFULLY

AS DRINK GOES INTO SEWER

Guthrie, Okla., Aug. I.—[Special.]--" What would they do with a fellow if he should put a tin cap under the end of that trough and get a drink of booze ? " The question was propounded by a Chicago travelling salesman as he watched the State Liquor Dispensary officials here during the last week emptying bottle after bottle, keg after keg, and gallon after gallon of boors, wines, and whiskies into a wooden trough, twelve feet long, that carried the liquor into a sewer. The different liquors mixed in the trough and flowed as one into the manhole, while the scent was wafted by the breeze for blocks down the street and caused still greater thirsty crowds to gither and watch the destruction.

The Chicago man's question remained unanswered. It was too solemn an occasion for the majority of those who were watching to talk. Some of them looked up expectantly, thinking perhaps that the problem might be solved so all of them could run for their tin cups. Finally, in desperation, the Chicago man cried aloud : " Will some one please tell me how I can get a drink ?"

- "Go see a doctor," was the reply of a bystander. " If there's anything about you that he can find that needs liquor as a medicine, you can got a prescription that will get you the whisky." Other travelling men had gathered, for the hour of emptying the liquor was comparatively early, and they were coming from breakfast at the hotel. It was a thoroughly disheartened crowd that heard the by-stander's answer' and there were tears in the voice of the man who spoke : " Well, that's the first time I ever saw anything as wicked as that." INFERIOR. "Booze" SPILLED. The liquors being destroyed had been confiscated by State and County officials throughout the State and shipped to dispensary headquarters in Guthrie. All of it had been examined and found to be below the State's standard for medicinal purposes, and under the pro- visions of the Billups prohibition law, it must be destroyed. Altogether there were 30,000 pint bottles of beer emptied, twenty-five kegs, several dozen cases of whisky and wines, and an occasional bottle of champagne. At one time, in fact, and it happened while the crowd of travelling men gathered, five kegs of beer, a big jug of whisky, a demijohn of wine, one of gin and several of beer, were flowing into the trough at the same time.

Down in the cellar, underneath the state dispensary building, an employs of the state is engaged in an apparently endless job of sorting real beer that has been seized in the raids from the other that is below the standard. Each bottle the man shakes and then holds it up to the light. If a cloud is visible ha throws the bottle to one side, as it is worthless. The bottles of good beer he places in another pile to be relabelled and sent out to the various local dispensaries throughout I the state to be sold for medicine only. The unpacking of the barrels of beer revealed many interesting things. In one instance the top of a barrel contained only a cheap grade of "tin top," while the lower portion was stored with a well-known brand of beer. Another barrel had a few bottles of near beer on top and below were cards, poker chips, and a jug of whisky. Still downward were located several dozen bottles of good beer. Such a great amount of the confiscated " bootleg " article of liquors had collected in the cellar that it became necessary to have a day of spilling in order to make room for the results of other raids.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
DITAIPING TIIE BOTTLED BEER.

Different methods are employed in emptying the many thousand bottles that are collected in raids. At the rear of the dispensary building a table with numerous holes bored in its top stands over a manhole. Several men are employed in palling corks and from them other men take the uncorked, foaming bottles and thrust the necks downward through the holes in the table's top. Here the bottles remain until thoroughly drained into the manhole beneath. the empty bottles are then stored away to be sold by the State for what- ever they will bring. Evidently the state expects the breweries to pur- chase the empty bottles, and there are enough empties on hand now at the dispensary to bring in revenue, at the prevailing price for empty bottles, enough funds to pay the salaries of several employs. Ia fact, it is said, this is the only portion of the state dispensary system that is providing anything like self-supporting.—From the San Francisco " Call."

MR. J. A. SEDDON M.P.

AT PENKETH.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
Tariff Reform No Remedy for Trade

Depression.

iMr. J. A. Seddon, M.P. addressed a public meeting in the Infants' School, Penketh, on Saturday evening. The gathering was to take place on the Recreation around, but the unfavourable weather compelled an adjournment indoors. Mr. J. Faulkner presided over a fair attendance, aud was supported by Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P., Councillor Radford (Buttonwood), and Dr. • Lathora (Earlestown.) Councillor Radford said the spirit of unity and brotherhood had begun to bind together the workers of the country, and if the growth of the Labour party con- tinued as hitherto, the two old-fashioned parties would become but dwarfs in comparison, and would have to give up that absolute control of the affairs of govern- ment which they had for centuries monopolised. The work which the Labour members had achieved in so short a time had been nothing short of marvellous, and their presence in the House had had a most vital and beneficial effect on the working classes. By their per- sistency they had forced the party in cline to introduce legislation for the good of the worker, and once it was introduced they had strenuously endeavoured to get the best possible results for the working classes. They had done a great deal towards converting the House of Commons from the gas works into the workshop of the nation. (Applause) Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P., who had a hearty reception, said the work of the last session had been much more beneficial than many people who simply took their politics from the newspapers would be led to believe. They had had discussions upon the proposals of the Licensing Bill and the Old Age Pensions Bill but several vital Bills which had either received the Royal assent or were awaiting their last stages in the autumn session had to some extent beau ignored. There was the children's Bill or

JUVENILE OFFENDERS' BILL. Ile was more than pleased that the Bill had progressed so far. It would be a safeguard to unfortunate children who might be found gailty of small petty larcenies. The way they had been treated in the past was a dis- grace to civilization. If the parent were poor, the boy had received a term of imprisonment and had been compelled to mix and fraternise with hardened criminals, with the result that very often the innocent boy who had committed a theft from love of sport or even hunger, had become a confirmed criminal. Under the Children's Bill that system would be done away with and there would be homes of detention where boys and girls would be taught habits of thrift and honesty. The

TOWN PLINNING BILL was a great step in advance of the old clumsy method which had hitherto been followed in building towns. Nlen whose only concern had been the making of profit had been allowed to build all sorts of hovels and to-day we had in England probably the greatest blot contained in any civilized country, so far as slams were concerned. The Town Planning Bill would give us healthier houses and better streets, and enable us to have some little beauty in towns as well as in the country. With regard to the

OLD AGE PENSIoNS BILL, even those who did not subscribe whole heartedly to the Labour party's programme would admit that the scheme had been considerably improved by the presence of the Labour party in the House of Commons. He noticed that the Tory Wain, Sir Ackland Hood, in a speech the previous took day credit to the Tory party for two amendment which had certainly improved the measure. The first question related to old married couples. He (Ir. Seddon) thought it was a very hard thing to assume that a couple living together at seventy years of age ought to be cantent with 7s. 6d. between' them whereas if they had been living apart they would have been entitled to ss. each. That was a serious blot on the scheme and the Labour party moved and succeeded in carrying an amendment whereby the old couple who had reached the age of seventy would be entitled to the full IN. between them. Now the Tory party had the audacity to claim the credit for it when all they did was to vote with the Labour party not for any love for the aged couple but because they wanted to get in a blow against the Government. Therefore they voted as a matter of party policy and not out of consideration for the interests of the aged poor. The other question was that of a man who had been thrifty and had saved a certain amount. Under the original form of the Bill, the man who had saved 10s. Id. a week from Friendly Societies or any other agency would not be entitled to any share in the pension scheme. He was glad the Labour party moved anct carried a graduated scheme whereby the man who had saved 10s. would be entitled to nn additional as., if he had saved Ils. to 45., 12s. to 35., 13s. to 25., and 14s. to Is., making 15s. the maximum. This was work done by the Labour party, and while looking superficially they might say that little had been done in the past session, if only the Acts to which he had referred were applied in the proper spirit, it wonld be a greater advance in social reform than any that had been attempted by other Governments during the last twenty years. (Applause).

' TIIE OUTLOOK AT PRESENT was not over-promising. We had discovered not only in this country but in Germany and America that the ebb and flow of depression in trade could overmouut the bnlwarks of Free frade or Protection. We were told by the tariff reformers that all our social ills would fade away if we adopted their nostrum. Lady Gerrard, speaking at a Primrose League meeting, made a very pretty speech—he might call it an essay—in which she said that what we wanted to do was to make the foreigner pay our rates and taxes and that we were to find work for our unemployed. That was a very enuring picture and if tariff reform would secure such advantages, the Free Trader would stand condemned as an enemy to his class and to his country. We knew from the experience of other countries that tariff reform was not the salvation of the worker. It was the very opposite. In Germany and America they were suffering from depression much more acute than we had in this country at the present time. We knew to some extent the causes of the depression we were suffering. The South African war was one of the wicked means of wasting money which brought about those periods of depression which came under Free Trade or Protection. fariff reform only aggravated social evils. Mr. Seddon pointed out Ithat a recent enquiry in Germany had shown that the German worker paid on the average 25 per cent, more in rent, received 18 per cent, less in wages, worked longer hours, and paid 15 to 25 per cent, more for food. Although it might have been said truthfully twenty- live years ago that Germany was the cheapest country to live in, to-day it was the dearest. If tariff reform benefited anyone, continued Nlr. Seddon, it was either the trustmonger or the landlord, lie could quite under- stand the landlords and the royalty lords asking for tariff reform, but he could not understand any working man giving his adherence to that policy. What was needed was an equitable system of taxation, so that by means of graduated income tax or increased death duties the community would be able to participate in the wealth they had created. At present the income of 5,000,00) of our population was greater thla that of the remaining 38,000,000. It was

A PRRFECT SCANDAL and a dishonest transaction that men who had done nothing to improve land and enhance its value should be able to levy tribute and toll on the activities of the people. He agreed with the taxation of land values as being equitable and right. Mr. Seddon instanced how the wealth of the Derby family had been enhanced by the growth and activity of the community which had increased the value of land and also gave a telling illustration of how the Duke of Bedford had reaped a large earn from a public improvement in London. The land belonged to the people by Divine law and human law and he looked forward to the time when it would be restored to its rightful owners. The Government's LICENSING DILL, said Mr. Seddon, was a wise, strong, statesmanlike effort to regain for the people their right with regard to the control of the drink monopoly. The future of the markets of the world would be with the temperate people, and it was wise statesmanship not to rob the people of their beer, but to educate them that too much indulgence in drink was not only destructive to their home happiness, but to the man as a wealth producer: He gave all credit to the late Colonel Pilkin,gton for what he bad done by the provisions of his will in regard to his own estate, but surely if it was within the right of a private landlord to say that there should be no public-houses on his estate, the community as a' whole ought to have an equal right to say whether they would or would not have public-houses. (Applause.) Mr. Foden moved a resolution expressing confidence in Mr. Seddon and declaring that no effective improve- ment could be secured except through a strong demo- cratic party in the House of Commons working to curb the monopoly in drink and land and secure through equitable taxation relief from the social ills which were a blot upon our national honour. Mr. Clay seconded and the resolution was carried unanimously. Dr. Lathom also addressed the meeting and expressed his pleasure that the Labour party had not had to resort to sports and alcoholic drinks for the purpose of cap- turing votes. The privileged classes could see the handwriting on the wall which declared that the day of land monopoly and mining royalties was coming to an ead. If a tithe of the money which the idle and luxurious people used was employed in research and investigation such calamities as the Abram disaster would be a thing of the past. A vote of thanks was accorded to the speakers on the proposition of the Chairman seconded by Mr. Loxley.

SILVER-STREET SCHOOLS.

At a meeting of the Street Improvement and Light- ing Committee an application from Mr. E. J. Guest on behalf of the Managers of Silver-street school to pur- chase from the Corporation rpiece of land in Silver- street large enough for w.c.'s was considered and refused. •

--- - Councillor HAWTHORN asked for an explanation of the refusal. He understood that the Board of Educa- tion had condemned the present sanitary conveniences and the managers had no land on which to erect new ones. If the matter was not attended to the schools would be condemned and it would mean a great expense to the Corporation IA they would have to provide a new school to accommodate 1,000 children. ' Councillor EVANS promised that if the matter was brought forward at another meeting of the Committee it would be fully gone into. SMOKE NUISANCE.

At a meeting of the Health Committee a letter was read from Miss Broadbent with reference to the nuisance caused by the emission of black smoke.. Councillor EVANS moved, and Alderman Btrirrox seconded, the passing, of the minutes. Alderman BENNETT expressed the hope that Mks Broadhent's letter would not be overlooked. Councillor EVANS replied that it was considered by the committee and a courteous answer was sent to Miss

Broadbent pointing out that the committee were deal- ing with the question on what they deemed to be fair lines without unduly hampering the traders of the town. ((lear, hear). The minutes were approved, including those contain- ing the scheme for the medical inspection of school children, and the new regulations for, slaughterhouses.

PRICE OF GAS REDUCED. At a meeting of the Gas Committee it was resolved that the price of gas be reduced by the sum of 2d. per 1,000 feet as from the Ist November next. Alderman PIERPOINT in moving the confirmation of the minutes, said the alteration of price meant,* reduc- tion of £2,650 per annum in tha revsnue. The prices now charged would be :—For the ordinary consumers 2s. 2d., for the larger consumers 2s. Id., and for especially large consumers 2s. The charge for power gas would be reduced from Is. Bd. to Is. 6d. He hoped the Council would unanimously confirm the minutes. Their profits in 1907 were £15,593, and they devoted to the relief of the rates a sum of £11,500, and transfered to the reserve account .£4,003, bringing up the reserve account to £lO,OOO. He contended that a reduction iu the price of gas meant increased consumption for lighting purposes, and would encourage the use of gas cookers and gas fres, both of which meant less smoke and a drawing nearer to the ideals of Alderman Bennett. The Connell should encourage that phase of the question as much as possible. (Hear, hear.) The reduction in the price of gas was a move in the night direction, (Hear, hear.) The Gas Committee had been making too large a prat, and the time had therefore arrived when the general consumers should share in it Alderman EVANS seconded.

Councillor SINGLETON said there was no doubt that the more they reduced the price of gas the more gas they would sell. The profits would be tbe same, and yet the consumer would get a benefit. The Corporation would lose nothing by the reduction. That had been proved in other towns. Councillor PEMBERTON said he hoped the Gas Com- mittee would allow those Rho used slot meters to participate in the reduction, because they were a large body of consumers. He was pleased to see that the price had come down for power also, and he belleved the profits of the Gas Committee would he increased in the long run. There was no town to approach Widnes in the matter of gas, and it was found there that by constantly reducing the price the more gas was used. Warrington was a working community, and most of the inhabitants were woi king men, and if they wished to find work for the rising generation and get works introduced into the town there must be reduc- tions in these and other ways. 'Warrington would compare favourably with any town in England with respect to electricity for power purposes, and if gas was placed on the same footing there was no reason why new works should not be brought to the town. (Hear, hear). The minutes were approved. THE WATER SUPPLY. • RAPID INCREASE IN CONSUMPTION.

Alderman EVANS proposed the adoption of the Water Committee's minutes, and said the Committee had resolved to instruct the Engineer to report upon the high level supply of water and the advisability of pro- viding some method of relieving the same. This was in consequence of the rapid increase iry the consumption of water. When the system was stalled in 1900 it was estimated that by 1918 they would require a supply of 170,000 gallons per day. Already they required double the quantity and had,. ten years to go. lhe demand at present was 330,000 gallons per day. The revenue from domestic water had increased from £1,003 in 1902 to £2,416 in 1908.- The question of the high level supply was a very important one and the Committee were going into it thoroughly to see if they could get out of a possible difficulty in the future. Councillor Ilourats seconded, and the minutes were adopted.

HINTS FOR TIIE HOME.

NERVOUS PROSTRATION.

Neurasthenia, or nervous prostration is simply exhaustion of the nervous vitality, and is pro- duced by any conditions which draw unduly upon the patient's strength. Overwork, worry, lack of sleep, with over or under-feeding, un- hygienic living, &c., are the usual causes. The only treatment of neurasthenia which promises good results is the hygienic. ' Two meals a day should be taken of light, nutritious food, consist- ing largely of fruits, nut foods, and cereals. A half-pint of hot water one hour before akh meal and free water drinking between mesqt, will be beneficial. Little or no fluids, howew r, should be taken with meals,. This treatrr ant, with proper baths, light, exhilarating outdoor exer- cise, and freedom from worry will, says the Family Doctor, usually effect a cure. The de- tails of treatment would, of course, depend upoa the peculiarities of the case, but these directions indicate the general plan.

BAKED APPLES.

So richly equipped in chemical elements is Ohs apple that it is recognised aS one of the easiesl vegetable substances to digest and is therefor* deservedly popular as h part of the daily menu. A baked apple is the most easily digested of any way of serving apples, bit there are baked apples and baked apples. Just to put the fruit in the oven and to cook with a suitable heat to tenderness is by no means the worst way of pre4 paring a baked apple, but there are various methods of seasoning and baking which servo variety, and variety serves the appetite mod pleasantly. One way is to peal the arMles before baking. Core the fruit, fill the hollows with sugar and any preferred spice. Lay in an enamelled baking pan, pour just ' enough water to cover the bottom, then bake in a moderate oven until tender and delicately crisp on the surface. A bit of butter put on the top of each apple adds •avoury flavour. Meanwhile take the cores and peelings, cover with water and stew for an hour. Strain, sweeten slightly, add a little grated lemon rind and pour over the apples. Whipped omarn will be liked with these apples, - .

There are a great many changes to be rung on The baked apple filling. Any kind of nute may be used. For a dozen apples a cupful and a-half of meets will be required. Chop fine and mix with as much sugar. Fill the cavities and bake as usual. A half banana E prink led with Bugs.z and lemon-juice makes a good filling. Figs and dates chopped and rolled in sugar, chopped nuts with chips of lemon or orange rind, or honey and butter, are all appropriate and plea. 'ant to the palate.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
TROUBLE WITH THE FEET.

Nothing is more- discouraging to the busy housewife than to have constant pain and di,- comfort in her feet. Often the trouble is aggro: vated by improper footwear. No one should buy shoes that are uncomfortable when new. Of all the pains that women _bear, and men, too, the most useless and foolish is pain in the feet. For tired feet, a hot foot-bath, with an ounce of salt in-44.4- restful. Put on fresh stockings and a change of shoes after taking it. The quickest relief from fatigue is to plunge the foot in cold eater, and keep it immersed there for a moment, or until there is a sensation of warmth. Another tonic for the sole is a handful of alcohol. This is a sure way to dry the feet after being out in the storm. Spirit baths are used by professional dancers, acrobats, and pedestrians to keep the feet in condition.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
NEW BREAD AND HOT ROLLS.

A word as to the unwholesomeness of new bread and hot rolls. 'When bread is taken out of the oven it is full of moisture; the starch, says Health, is held together in masses, and the bread, instead of being crusted so as to expose each grain of starch to the saliva, actually pre- vents their digestion by ,being formed by the teeth into leathery masses, which lie on the stomach like so many bullets. Bread should al- ways be at least a aay or two old before it is eaten, and if properly made and kept in a cool, dry place, ought to be perfectly soft and palat- able at the end of three or four days. Hot rolls with plenty of butter should be carefully shunned by everyone who has the slightest re- spect for the -stomach. Flour, when freshly ground, is too glutinous to snake good bread, and should, therefore,. not be used immediately. but should be kept dry for a few weeks, and stirred occasionally until it crumbles easily be.• tween the fingers.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
CHEA.P GIBE.

It would be too 0:1113bL to expect that Opposition journals would repA tho temptation to fling a cheap and superficial taunt against the Chancellor of the Exchequer for having undertaken an inquiry into German old age pensions after the Government's own scheme had been placed upon the statute-book. Of course, the German systera had been, with many others, the subject of careful investigation by experts and economists, and by the Committees which have sat to examine the feasibility of old age pensions in this country. The solution of the problem had not been lacking so long for want of study, but for want of resolution in statesmen to apply the results of such study. We had the Aberdare Committee and the Rothschild Committee, and these were followed by a Select Committee of the Elguse of Commons, which came to the conclusion that a system of old age pen- sions was p-acticable on a non contributory basis. As a member of that Committee, Mr. Lloyd George was naturally familiar with all the essential features of the German contributory system. But a personal inspection (4 the actual operations of the machinery presented obvious advantages, while it was still more important to hear and weigh at first hand the verdict passed upon the system by representatives of the German working men, and to learn all that could be learnt about the extensive developments which are contemplated.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
INSURA.NCE IN GEFZIIANY.

The large scale of these projected developments can best be realised by the aid of a few figures. At pre- sent the contributions of employers and working men t,gether towards insurance against old age, sickness and "invalidity," amount to £31,030,000, and the Government contribution to the working expenses is two and a half millions. In 1910, or therealeouts, it is anticipated that the inclusion of some classes, as yet unaffected, will bring the fund up to 53,000,000, with a Government contribution of five millions. There is a general agreement in Germany that the results of insurance have proved beneficial. Socialist criticism is chiefly in the direction of complaints that the pensions are too low, and of a wish to see the Government entering on a crusade of preventive work against alcoholism and industrial diseases. Mr. Lloyd George has expressed a plain opinion that old age pensions should be folio red up by the provision of a sick and invalid fund; but he would not be prepared to generalise from the example of Germany as to the lines on which such a fund should be constituted. There are other countries - and particularly Austria and 'Belgium that can furnish data not less valuable.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PRINCIPLE OF THE PATENTS ACT.

There is an almost universal agreement that the Patents Act is benefiting, and will in future benefit to a much higher degree, the industries of this country. !et thee is a good deal of apprehension both as to the purpose and the operations of the measure. There are some persons who, through misconceiving its character, fear that it may' increase the price of certain patented articles to the consumer. And there are others who, through the same I( ind of misconcep- tion, attribute to it what they believe to ha virtues, but what weald, in reality, be vi( The Act does not tend, as is' sometimes thought, to restrain trade that would manifestly be a vicious tendency, though it would be welcomed by the adv( •ates of Tariff Reform - but on the contrary it breaks down the monopoly of those syndicates and companies which I used to avail themselves of the unsatisfactory state of our patent law to prevent British manufacturers from producinr, articles that they themselves had no intention of producing here, or to prevent thi3 free import into this country of the articles for which they had taken 'out a purely obstructive patent. It is just this obstructive class of patents that the Act is aimed against. A patent is a kind of monopoly with a time limit, which is granted to an inventor for the purpose of stimulating the development of improved methods of production. Experience demonstrated the existing patent system in this country to be so faulty that instead of stimulating improvement of methods, it merely si ured to many patentees a monopoly which was altogether injurious to the commenity at large. The new Act strikes at the root of this evil by render- ing a patent that is not worked in this country liable to revocation, and by compiling the patentee to satisfy the reasonable requirements of the public, ntt only by manufacturing to an adequate extent, but also by supplying the patented article on reasonable terms. There cannot be the shadow of a Protectionist taint in a measure which breaks down monopoly where it is harmful to the consumer, since the essence of Pr( tection is to sacrifice the consumer to the monopolist. But the Conservatives are anxious to snatch at some fancied consolation for the galling contrast between the zeal of the Liberal Government for the encourage- ment of industry and the apathy displayci by the previous Government, which could offer the British manufacturer nothing better than the discredited nc:;trum of Protection.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
CAPITAL GOING ABROAD.

--------- The value of the Patents Act, it will be seen, is not to be measured by the number of factories which are set up here by foiaign capitalish in order to avoid the danger of having their patents revoked. Its chief value consists in the elimination of merely obstructive patents. Still, we may feel a legitimate .tisfactiol at tlre sight of factories being erected here with foreign capital, though this is, as the British Consul-General at Frankfort has pointed out, sometimes due t) other considerations than the desire to retain a British patent. German manufacturers have discovered that it is an advantage to carry on their works in a country where prices are not epormously enhanced by protective duties. There is something very quaint about the anxiety which many Conserva- tives are now evincing lest 13ritish capital is L hog driven abroad by the atti..ction of getting inside the foreign tariff wall, and by the dread of Socialist " legislation. When the fiscal controversy began, Protectionists raised the outcry that we were paying I for our imports by selling out our investments held abroad. They are• determined, it seems, that we shall I be in trepidation about our capital, but they cannot make up their minds whether the appalling fate which is overtaking it is that it is all going abroad, or that it is all being returned to this conntry in the shape of imports.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0085.txt | included=true | included =====
CONSERVATIVE DISCOVERIES.

The Conservative Party must be suffering from the complaint which oculists call preshyopia—the faulty eyesight found in some old people whdare so long- sighted that they cannot distinguish objects clearly t t come within any practical range. Lord Hugh ecil can look far away into the future, and make out with confidence the success of the Unionist Party's principles. Bat he might' be very much at a loss if he were asked to define what its principles are now , in relation to tariff reform.. He can turn his gaze ' back upon the past and tell us what a great pirty the Liberal Party used to be. But iv hen he attempts to explain in what he finds the decay of that Party , to-day, he can only murmer that it has not put , forward the principles of religious equality," and that the Licensing Bill will never Lave , the consent and co-operation of , the brewers. Lord Londonderry had also a very. odd remark —odds that is,,for a Conservative politician—to make about the Licensing Bill.and the Education Bill. He censures the Bishops for their inconsistency in suy porting the one and opposing the other. It is, of course, inconsistent at one time to maintain the right of the nation to 9ake the general good predominate over the selfishness of vested interests, and at another to subordinate the general good to sectional interests, even if those interests are vested in an Established Church. But that is not what Lord Londonderry meant. Mr. Runciman has declared that there can only be settlement of the education question on one basis, and that is the basis of religious equality. It might have been thought that this declaration would have reassured Lord Hugh Cecil as to the intention of the Government to maintain its traditional principles, for which he expresses so touching an anxiety. RELIGIPUS EQUALITY. But Lord Hugh Cecil understands religious equality in a very iiteculiar sense. He understands it in that " strict sense," according to which Mr. Balfour was able to assert at the meeting of the Parents' League in July, that the Education Act of 1002 alleviated

ever*, Nonconformist grievance, and that religious tests have never been imposed on teachers. Those who have been persuaded by Hr. BiHour that the Act of 1032 was a great alleviation of Nonconformist grievances, vill hive no difficulty in discovering, with Loid Hugh Cecil, a great falling off, in the present Government's policy from the principles of religious equality. No Conservative speaker can touch upon the Education question nowad tys,without bringing in the necessity of preserving the right of the parent to ' choose the creed in which his children shall be educated. Sir A. Acland-Llood put forward his plea for it at Wellington, and Lori Hugh Cecil at Wynyard Park. But before they set themselves to the task of proving that such a right ought, at all costs, to he maintained, they should attempt to show that such a right exists etre idy. MIT can it be said to exist in the large number of those single-school areas where Nonconformist parents have t 3 choose between sending their children to Church schools or letting them go without any religious instruction at all ?

There was once a shepherd who wished to get married; but, although he knew three sisters, each one was as pretty as the others, and the choice was therefore so difficult that he did not know to which one to give the preference. Se he asked his mother's advice, and she told him to invite all three of them to supper and tcp place a cheese before them and observe how they cut it. The youth did so; and the first sister ate her cheese, rind and all; the second cut off the rind so hastily that she cut with it some of the good cheese and threw it all away, but the third sitter pared the rind-off very care- fully, neither too much nor too little. The shepherd, thereupon, told all to his mother, and he said, "Take the youngest sister.". He did so, and lived contentedly and happily with her all his life long.

- MAGGIE EASTVVOOD. BROUGHT HER A FORTUNE.

A short time ago a nian died in Brussels leaving nearly his entire fortune to a young woman who was entirely unacquainted with him. This is how it cam oto pass: He was a very eccentric man, and set out, like Diogenee, in search of an honest man. His tub was au omnibus, and his lantern a small coin.

In the omnibus he took his seat every day near the conductor, and always showed himself very obliging in passing up the money c f pas- sengers and returning the change; but to the latter he always managed to add a. franc ,or a half franc. Then he would watch those to whom it came. They would count it carefully, notice the extra coin, and invariably slip it into their pockets. No one thought of the poor conductor, whose meagre salary of only three francs a day could ill support sUch a loss. But at last a young woman passed her's back, with: "Conductor, you have given me half a franc toq much." Diogenes, delighted, fol- lowed her home, made inquiries, and a; the answers were satisfactory, made his will in her favour, though he never gave her warning that her half franc was going to bring her a million sterling..

[Many thanks for drawing received.—UNcLE in.) • —4..... ' A DRUNKARD'S CLOAK.

This curious survival of the past came under the hammer a short time ago. Before it was put up for competition a man was found .to allow himself to be encased in the wooden crinoline,. and submit to the ordeal of being photographed in- it. The ".drunkard's cloak" was` hung• from the victim's neck inst vd ,of the waist. It was, in fact, the ancient counter- part of the strait waistcoat. In shape it le- sembles a huge inverted flower-pot. The small circular hole in which the victim's neck was locked gave him little margin for movement— only sufficient room to prevent strangulation. There the victim waS imprisoned. The weight of this wooden cloak, which /cached almost to the ground, would drag down his shoulders till every bone ached. ,

NAPOLEON'S CHAIR.

Hem indeed, is one of the seats ,of the mighty. It is a chair used by Napoleon. It afterwards became the property of Horace Walpole,, and is riow in the possession of a distinguished Englishman. It ie guarded as though it were made of gold, and because of its dual association is doubly valued. It was one of the most conspicuous pieces in a recent exhibition, but when one realises that Napoleon must have sat upon Many, many chairs in his career, it is easy to see that the production of this furniture may become Quite a profitable industry. However, this chair is entirely authentic.

POSTAL AN) SAVNB RANK RIDLITIONS For further particulars see "Post Office Guide," at any Post o.fice. POSTAGE STAMPS. , Books containing 12 penny and 23 halfpenny staaipsare now on sale at each post office. Price 2s. INLAND CORRESPONDENCE. The term "Inland Correspondence " means cor- respondence posted within the United kingdom, including the Isle of Man and the Channel Islands. "'lnland Correspondence" is divided into the following classes : (a) Letters (d) Newspapers (h) Postcards (e) Parcels (c) Halfpenny packets Not only letters, but any document or article (with a few trifling exceptions) which can be trans- mitted by post at all, may be sent by letter post, the only limitation being that of size. LETTER POST. The prepaid rate of postage is as follows : Not exceeding 4 ozs. in weight ... Id. For every additionai 2 0z5.... 11.‘ A letter posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with double postage;, a letter insufficiently paid, with double the deficiency. No letter may exceed two feet in length, one foot in width, or one font in depth. Post-CARDS. The prepaid rate of postage on every post-card is one half-penny, and on every reply post-card one penny. A post-card posted unpaid is chargeable on delivery with one penny. Cards used as post-cards must be madeof ordinary card-board, not thicker than the material used for the thickest official post-card. They may not exceed 51 inches in length by 31 incites in width, or be less than 31 inches in length by 21 inches in width. A gummed label (not to exceed 2 inches long and finch wide) bearing the address may now be aflixed to the address side of card. If any of the rules relating to a post-card be infringed, the card will be treated as a letter. HALFPENNY Pam= POST. The Halfpenny Packet Post can be used only for packets not exceeding 20z. in weight. Printed matter, Scc., not in the nature of a letter, • may be transmitted by this method. NEWSPAPER POST. The prepaid rate of postage on a registered news- paper, including any supplement thereto, whether posted singly or with others is 0. Newspapers not registered at the General Post Office are treated in the pcst like any other printed matter. PARCEL POST. The rates of postage on parcels areas follows: Fora parcel ... not exceeding I lb. 0 3 „ • exceeding 1 lb. 2 lb. 0 4 ft f 1 9 lh • JO 3 lb. 0 5 ft ff 3 lb. 5 lb. 0 6 tI „ 5 lb. a 7 lb. 0 7 • ft 7 lb. 8 lb. 0 8 8 lb. 91b. 0 9 9 lb. 10 lb. 0 10 10 lb. • ~11 lb. 0 11 No p• arcel may exceed 111 b. weight. The size allowed for an inland parcel Is : Greatest length— ... 3 ft. 6 in. Greatest length and girth combined 6 ft. 0 in. For example—A parcel measuring 3ft. 6in, in its longest dimensions as much as 2ft. Gin, round its thiakest part ; a short parcel may be thicker • thus, if it measure no more than 3ft, in length, it May measure as much as 3ft. round its thickest part. The postage on a parcel must be prepaid. Mark each parcel" Parcel Post." A parcel must not be posted in a letter box, but presented at the counter of a post office, and the sender should see that the weight, size and postageare in order before leaving. PARCELS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL). Parcels are accepted for parcel post to most foreign countries under conditions simildr to the inland regulations, except that for customs require- ments the contents must be declared. The customs charges can be prepaid or collected on 'delivery. The postage varies to different countries and must be prepaid. MONEY ORDERS (INLAND.) The postal authorities cannot issue a single money order for more than £4O, and will only cash them at the money order office at which they are made payable, unless they are crossed, when they can be paid through a bank. No fractional part of a Id. can be contnined in a money order. The rates of poundage are :—Not exceeding £l, 23. ; £3, 3d.; £lO, 41.; £2O, 63. ; £.30, Bd. ; £4O, 101 By paying an additional 2d. and cost of the telegram of advice, a money order can be telegraphed under certain • restrictions. MONEY ORDERS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL.) The maximum amount for which a money order .may be issued for places abroad varies in each particular place. The poundage rates are :—Not exceeding £1,33. ; £2, 6d. ; and above that., 3d. for each additional £2 or portion of it. A foe of 63. in addition to the ordinary poundage, together with cost of telegram of advice, enables money orders to be telegraphed to certain places abroad. POST.AL ORDERS. These are obtainable at any money order office in the United kingdom, and possess the advantage over money orders that they can be cashed at any money order office. If crossed,- they can be paid into a bank. They are issued for fixed amounts, rising by ad., from 6d. to £1 Is. The poundage fees are up to 2s. ad.; Id. up to 155.; Id. up to 21s. Before parting with a postal order, always fill in the name of the person to whom the amount is to be paid. Each order is numbered, as is also the counterfoil attached, which latter sbould be retained and name of payee filled in. The Post- master-General. will entertr.in no further claim salter an order is once paid. Stamps to the amount of sd. can be affixed to make up odd sums. TELEGRAMS (INLAND). The Postmaster-General possesses,almost eptirely, the privilege of transmitting,collecting and deliver- ing telegrams in the United Kingdom. The charge for a telegram is a minimum amount of ficl., which covers 12 words, and an additional I.d. for each extra word. This charge includes delivery within the postal district if sent out for delivery from a head telegraph office, hut if from a sub-office then within three miles only, beyond which a charge of 3d. per mile is made from the office- door. All charges are, as far as possible, prepared by affixing stamps to telegram form. Address of person receiv- ing a telegram has to be paid for, lint sender's name if written on back of form need not be. Five figures count as one word. The cost of a reply may be prepaid. Telegrams can be repeated oa payment of half the original charge, which extra payment - is refunded if error is detected. Telegrams may be posted in wall or pillar boxes, or handed to rural postmen, and are wired as soon as received at the nearest telegraph office. Late fees secure attention, in certain cases, to telegrams handed in after the usual hours. The cost of registering an abbrevi- ated address, which must consist of two words, is £1 IS. TELEGRAMS (FOREIGN AND COLONIAL) Must be writ ten on special forms and are accepted In the language of any of the principal European countries. The rates vary, and are too numerous to detail here. POST OFFICE SAVINGS BANK. Theme are established at all money order offices. In ordinary cases the annual deposit must not exceed £5O, and the total amount £2OO. No person can have more than one account. Amounts placed in the bank on behalf of children under seven years of age cannot be withdrawn until the child reaches that age, and then only under his or her own signa- ture. The smallest amount received at a time is Is., and this may take the form of 12 penny stamps affixed to a special form. Each depositor receives a bank book in whioh the amount of each deposit should be entered at the time of paying in. If the deposit is £1 or over a special acknowledgment is sent from Overhead London office. Interest is allowed at the rate-of 2i per cent. Any amount up to and including £1 may be withdrawn without previous notice, on presenting a bank book. The bank book should be sent each year, postage free, on the anniversary of the date when the account was opened, to the Controller of the Savings Bank Department for purposes of checking and adding interest. The withdrawal of money can be expedited by telegraphing, the depositor paying cost of telegrams. P.O. TELEPHONE AND TRUNK T.INE CHARGES. The fees for the use of the Trunk lines are as follows:— For 25 miles or under ... 3d. ta 50 • „ 1, 75 if ff „ 100 „ „ „ every additional 40 miles or fraction thereof... ... Between the hours of 7 p.m. and 7 an., six minutes conversation is allowed for the same price as three minutes between 7 a.m. and 7 p.m., and a reduced charge is made for three minute calls. MOTOR CAR AND CYCLE REGULATIONS. Each one must be registered with the council of a county or county borough, the fee being £l. for a motor car, and ss. for a motor cycle. A registered number is assigned to each, which it is an offence not to keep affixed when driving. Each driver must possess a license, costing 55., to be renewed every 12 months, and obtainable from the county authorities, which may be endorsed or suspended for reckless driving or certain other offences. The highest speed at whicla a car may be driven on the puk,lic highways is 20 miles an hour, which is reduced to 10 miles under special local regula- tions. It is an offence under the Motor Car Act (1903), to drive a oar in such a way on a public highway as to be dargerous to the public, having regard to all the circumstances of the case. In the event of an accident to any person due to the presence of the motor car on the read. the driver must supply his name and address, and the owner and number of the car, under heavy penal- ties. The driver must produce his licence at the , request of a police constable. '

THE TAXATION OF LAND VALUES.

On Saturday night an open-air meeting at Bridge foot, Warrington, was addressed by Mr. JOHN BACOT of St. Annes-on-Sea in support of the taxation of Land Values, lie said the single taxers were the friends of all parties and all social reformers, in-as-much as they desire to place the finances of the country on a sound economict basis. Taxation could only be drawn from either land or labour and so long as the country neglected the former source, social reform oould not proceed. The reason for this was, that in all the schemes of municipalising and nationalising which were called ,social reform' wealth was withdrawn from productive 'purposes and devoted very largely to non-productive purposes. The fact remained, that we are no better off for all the expenditure, they are just as many poor people and it is quite as hard to get a living as ever it was. The reason for this was, that all public and private improvements increased (economic rent. As to the—remedy the audience were asked not to be misled by people who told them, that the Govern- ment could do this and that for them by getting money from the rich through income taxes and death duties or by what some politicians call taxing the foreigner. There was no one who had anything to give theta and if they had they would not give it. The only way by which social reform could be accomplished was to open I natural opportunities free to all. As land was the source of unemployment, land must be put to use and in order to compel the use of land there was no better I method than that of a progressive tax on land values. The success achieved in our colonies in Germany and else was inslanced, and the speaker concluded with an earnest appeal to bis audience to ally themselves with tlio Warrington Braneh of the English League. for the Tax ttion rif Land Values, so that Warrington might take its place in a forthcoming struggle for this great reform. _

11Tr.11. OCKEL also addressed some pertinent remarks to the gathering, one of which was, that according to Prof. Thorold Rogers, in the 15th century, a common labouring man could with 15 weeks work of 8 hours a day, support his family of five all the year round, and an artisan even with 11 weeks work, that meant in the former case .£l4O a year in to-day's money. If we now consider that several horse-power in engines are work- ing to-day foievery human being, and that the manifold inventors have enormously increased the productive power of men, they must come to the conclusion that the lowest paid man is earning and would receive under the single tax on land values at least £l,OOO a year.

COUNCIL.

HEALTII OF THD DISTRICT. Dr. SEPHTON reported that during the month thero had been 14 births and 14 deaths. The death-rate was equal to 11.5 per 1,000 against 29 the average for the last three months, and 1513 for thesorrespondinr,° month of last year. Nine deaths occurred in the County Asylum, so that the death-rate proper for the district was 48 per I,ooo—a very low rate, especially for this time of the year. The infectious diseases notified were :—Diptheria, one case ; scarlet fever, one case. The report was adopted. TlI E ICSTIMATES.
