LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS. Authors of letters intended for publication tunAt in all cases send tht=ir names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. ,

Beautiful Warrington Society. ( Sir,—At the present time the B.W.S. is prominently ' brought before the public, by the splendid efforts put forth to bring about the creditable floral display on the occasion of the 3rd annual show. Much has been done since the formation of the Society and all praise is due to those who have laboured to improve and beautify our town. The exhibition at the Drill Hall on Satur- day last proves that it is possible to grow the most beautiful plants and flowers within the borough, in spite of the smoky atmosphere. Ti my mind the society is still in its infancy, and much remains to be done. The Chairman and others said at the opening ceremony that the number of patrons and exhibitors, leaves much to be desired. The people do not yet seem to realise that the effort is for them to share ; that the smallest front or back garden, window-box or window-plant can be entered for competition. It is pleasing to note that there is a considerable improvement in the number of small front gardens cultivated, but there are still many utterly neglected. A little help, encouragement and instruction might alter this and create a desire to beautify even the dismal back yards by the growth of shrubs and flowers. It is surprising what a change can be brought about in this direction. To my mind a personal canvass of likely householders in the town, would do more in a few months, than years of patient waiting ; this has been proved, time after time, in commercial, political, religious, and social enterprises. It could be carried out by a small committee in each ward. The distribution of booklets (as published by the One and All Association) to those becoming members would be helpful, also occasional meetings for lectures aud study in gardening, &c. It is the primary instruction that is mostly required to add further success to this movement, and I trust that something more will be done to increase the popu- larity of the Society's work. Yours truly, F. MORRALL.

C 3. Orford-avenue, Warrington.

"Daylight Saving Bill." Sir,—Absence from Warrington has not permitted my dealing earlier with the letter contained in your issue of the l Sth ult. relative to the Warrington Chamber of Commerce having passed a resolution in favour of the above Bill. A genuine examination by its writer of the proposals therein embodied would have avoided the necessity for the greater portion of his objections, as under them Greenwich mean time will continue to form" the basis from which time all over the world is reckoned." No doubt your astronomical but anonymous corres- pondent thinks like Sir Robert Ball as the latter humorously remarked, when giving evidence before the Select Committee appointed to inquire into the matter, that "daylight is a mistake altogether" but equally like him, I hope, will on consideration sympathise with the objects in view and agree that to bring more sun- shine into people's lives and thereby make the nation stronger, happier and healthier, is no bad form of patriotism. Brieff y, the effect of the proposals of the Bill would be:- 1. To move the usual hours of work and leisure nearer to sunrise. 2. To promote the greater use of daylight for recreative purposes of all kinds. 3. To lessen the use of licensed houses. 4. To facilitt.te the training of the Territorial

Forces. 5. To reduce the industrial, commercial and domestic expenditure on artiftial light. 6. To benefit tke physique, general health and welfare of all classes of the community. When these advantages can be obtained, not only without expense, but at an estimated annual saving equivalent to four-flfths of the annual interest on one hundred millions sterling of the National Debt, there is no single worlier in this country who should not endeavour to further a measure which has already received the approval of so many well-known men repre- senting science, industry and the professions, to say nothing of more than two hundred members of the Lords and the Commons. If the writer of the letter is inclined to disclose his identity 1 shall be only too pleased to discuss the matter further with him, either privately or publicly—l have, Sir, the honour to remain,HOLMAN KINGDON, (President Warrington Chamber of Commerce.)

===== 0002601_19080905_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
Tariff Reform.

Sir,—l have rend with interest the various reports ol the rceetiegs of the Warrington Tariff Reform League. At the last gathering Alderman Bennett is reported to have dealt with my letter to the Press. As no report is given of what the worthy Alderman said I am unable to offer any comment. I would therefore deal with the general line of argument used at these and similar meetings. The main line of thought can be expressed as follows : Goods imported, if kept out by a tariff and made in this country, would employ a great number of English workmen and the amount paid in wages would he a gain to this country. We must keep our money in the country. If the speakers allow the con- tention of Frea Traders respecting the increase of price to the consumer, they contend that there would still be a large balance in favour of their policy. Suppose goods imported value £9O could be produced here for £lOO they argue there would be a gain of £9O to the nation. So from every platform this £9O, or its equivalent (which is looked upon as an extra amount of wages to the British workmen) is dangled before the audience to secure their adhesion to so-called "Tariff Reform." The speakers generally take some trade in which their hearers are interested, give the amount of manufactured or partially manufactured goods we import, and then ask the question" What does this mean to the workers? It means unemployment and an empty cupboard for the little ones." It may be contended by such speakers that the audience they address are not capable of appreciating the principle of exchange upon which all trade is based, and therefore they suppress it. They may ask if you were to teach the principles of dynamics to a street audience would you tell them that "every atom in the universe attracts every other atom with a force ,--cerertionatetoitS weight and to the square of its dis- Lance- ?-15 -11 ut it ia no Mare pouiblo in dpiarnics to Ignore the law of gravitation than it 1§ inttre national trade to ignort 'pri:rellii; of o*Xcliange, And this is what almost every Tariff Reform speaker does. The arguments used not only ignore it but by implication deny it, else why speak of "money going out of the country " just as if we paid for our imports In cash. I said almost every Tariff Reformer ignores the principle. There are exceptions, Mr. G. L. Garvin an able Tariff Reformers says "It is true that every import must develop a corresponding export." He goes on to argue that while it is true that imports are paid for by exports, that imports develop exports, Tariff Reform could be made to affect the character of the imports. This a very interesting phase of the argu- ment, but as it has not, as far as I know, been put forward in Warringten we may put it aside for the pre- vent and confine ourselves to local arguments. If as Mr. Garvin truly states every import develops a corresponding export what about " the unemployment, the empty cupboard, the food for the little ones " of those engaged in the export of the goods or the service rendered which pays for the goods which the Warring- ton Tariff Reformers would exclude? The £9O, spoken of in an earlier part of this letter, would only mean a transfer from one set of workers to another and the £lO representing the increased cost together with the wasted labour in collection of the taxes would be the extent of the national loss which would have to be borne almost in its entirety by the workers. CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh, September 3rd, 1903.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MUSICAL SOCIETY'S OPERA.

" Morrie England."

As will be seen from an announcement in our advertising columns the Warrington Musical Society's sixth operatic venture is now within measurable dis- tance. " Merrie England," which brought both its composer, Edward German, and the librettist, Basil ' Hood, into front rank as collaborists in light opera, is to be given by members of the society in the middle of October. The rehearsals, suspended since the third week in July, have now been resumed and great enthusiasm is felt on the part of all concerned— principals, zhorus and orchestra,—in the progress of the piece and in regard to its success with the public. It will be remembered, that this year the performances will be given in the Royal Court Theatre where the ample stage accommodation and the facilities for scenic display will doubtless be material helps towards what is confidently expected will be a triumphant success. The scale on which " Merrie England " will be presented for the first time in Warrington will be in every respect adequate and com- plete, special scenery and new costumes having been requisitioned for the occasion. In this connection it is interesting to note that the people in the cast, the chorus and the orchestra will make up 100 performers. Mr. Frank H Crossley will again occupy his accustomed post as Musical Director and Conductor and Mr. Foden Williams is again this year the opera company's coach and stage manager. The plan of reserved seats will open at the Royal Court Theatre on Wednesday morn- ing September 30th at half-past eight by which time the illustrated souvenir programme and summary of the opera is expected to be published.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Aukland—Clayton.

A wedding of considerable local interest took place at Tlaelwall Church on Tuesday, when Mr. Leonard , Aukland, son of Mr. Ephraim Aukland, Froghall-lane, was married to Miss Margaret Clayton, eldest daughter of Mr. Samuel Clayton, of Warrington. The vicar (Rev. T. Pym Williamson) officiated. The; bride was given away by her uncle, Mr. James Clayton, ; of Laurel Bank, Thelwall. She was charmingly attired in a cream serge costume ttimmed with silk braid, and white lace hat trimmed with chiffon and lilies of the valley, and carried a bouquet of white . roses, lilies and sweet peas. The bridesmaids -were ; Miss Dela Bond and Miss Georgina Carter, who wore pink-muslin dresses in the Empire style, with hats to match. Their bouquets were composed of pink sweet peas and were the gifts of the bridegroom. Mr. Ernest Clayton (uncle of the bride) discharged the duties of best man. After the ceremony a reception was held at the house of the bride's uncle, Ur. James Clayton, Laurel Bank, Thelwall, and later the happy couple left for Blackpool where the honeymoon will be spent. The bride's travelling dress was a sax() blue co,tume with black picture hat, trimmed with ostrich plumes. Numerous handsome presents were received.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

The season opened on Thursday uhen the first Northern Union match was played at Hull, where Bradford suffered defeat by .13 points to nil.

To-day Warrington shake the dust off their boots and visit Leigh to play a match which counts in three competitions : The Northern League, the Lancashire League, and the South-West Lancashire League. How they will fare is somewhat problematical, but in recent years they have asserted considerable superiority over their neighbours, and with bcth sides composed of familiar players there should be plenty of confidence in the Warrington men. Their win of 26 points to nothing last season should inspire them to brilliant efforts.

The following team has been selected, and is the best available : Tilley, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson, and Taylor, threequarters ; Lloyd and Brooks, half backs; A. Boardman, P. Boardman, Naylor, Belton, Jolley and Swift, forwards.

The only weakness on paper is forward, where such men as Thomas, Shugars and F. G. Jenkins will be much missed until they return from the land of the leek. The present forward line has certainly an old- fashioned look, but the men at least possess weight ' and expeiience and ought to be able to hold the scrums unless Leigh have got a very hot front rank this season. Warrington must try to make the game as open as possible, and the backs should be good enough to get the points.

Leigh's team will be : Clarkson, full back ;S. Johnson, T. Jchnson, Bennett and Neville, three- quarters ; It. Bate and 'Whittaker, half-backs, Ashton, Gallop, McMaster, Webster and Jones, forwards.

With three Warrington men on the home side the game should be interesting enough to attract a good crowd from both districts.

With so much signing on by NIT. clubs, it is pleasant to announce that Warrington have not been idle. They have secured the signature of Edgar Harris, a wing three-quarter from Blaenavon, S.W. Harris is only 21 years old, stands aft. 9ins. high, and weighs 10 stones 12Ibs. He has the reputation of being a sprinter, and in one trial match he played here he showed that he could 4travel at a speed out of the ordinary. Be is down to assist the "A" team to-day, and a half back may also come with him. Mclntyre, of Stockton Heath, has also signed on, and will act as full-back for the " A " team.

The " A " team plays Seedley Hornets at Warring ton and will be represented by AlcinLyre, fall back;

Landor, Jordan, Cartwright and Harris, threequarters, Daintith and Another, half backs • Harmer, Bradshaw, Kettle, Cheaters, Hewitt and Fairhurst, forwards.

Season Tickets are now on sale and a great demand has already been made for them, and the Treasurer confidently expects that a record membership will be obtained.

Cricket.

There was plenty of excitement in the match between Warrington and Wigan at Arpley on Saturday and until the last few minutes it was impossible to tell who would win. The victory rested with the Warringtonians but there was only a matter of three runs between the teams. The home players are to be congratulated on their performance for the whole eleven worked wonder- fully well and the fielding was brilliant. A noteworthy feature of the game was the bowling of B. Silcock, who in the course oT five overs took six wickets for seven runs. To him much of the credit for the victory is undoubtedly due.

W. Bainbridge was again in first rate form with the bat and his 25 was faultlessly compiled.

St. James's eleven are to be acclaimed champions of the Warrington and District League, and they are -deserving of every praise on having got through the season without a single defeat. On Saturday they easily accounted for Cheshire Lines Engineers who for the second year in succession occupy the position of the runners-up. Holt and Hallam bowled splendidly, taking three wickets for five runs, and six wickets for six runs respectively. C. Holt with 19 was the top scorer, while J. Sherratt and J. Lester each made 18, the latter being not out.

In the Warrington and District League, Orford gave Holy Trinity a severe drubbing. A. Clare was in flne form with the willow and amassed 67, and W. Jolley contributed a valuable 31.

Walker's Brewery justified their position as champions of the Manchester and District Brewery League by handsomely defeating an eleven repre6enting the rest of the League. The Warringtonians ran up a majority of 30 with two wickets in hand, a feature of the encounter being the bowling of W. Norman who accounted for five wickets for ii runs. J. Cain (19) was the highest scorer.

Latchford Baptists severely trounced Monks, Hall and Co., A. Bramhall batting in grand style for his 45 not out. Not one of the forge eleven attained double figures.

Bowling.

There is litte of importance to chronicle in the bowling world. Local interest is chiefly concentrated on the annual handicap in connection with the Works Association which is now in Pull swing. The first round was completed on Saturday and the second stage will be entered upon to-day. Last Saturday's scores, together with the draw for the second round, will be found in another column.

The cup presented to the Constitution Lodge of Oid- fellows by P.P.G.M. Thomas Steel is a very handsome trophy and there will doubtless be keen competition a fortnight to-day for the honour of being the firs' holder.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0122.txt | included=true | included =====
Harriers.

The annual general meeting of the Warrington Wesleyan Harriers was hold on Tuesday evening at the Silver-street schoolroom, when about 30 members were present. All arrangements for the coming season are now in hand, and practice runs are to be held from the schoolroom, Silver-street, on the evenings of September 15th and 24th, the opening Saturday run being held from the same place on October 3rd, when a good turn out is expected. Mr. H. Gill was elected captain, be having as his assistants Messrs. Massey and Gibson. Fixtures are being arranged with teams from Manchester, Liverpool, Runcorn, St. Helens, and Northwich, and a prosperous season is being anticipated. Anyone wish- ing to join will be welcomed on any of the above dates or any Saturday during the season.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. -- Mr. A. J. Jolley's Re-appearance.

THE ESTIMATES,

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, under the presi- dency of Mr. G. Mather. There were also present : Mrs. Wilkinson, Mrs. Simpson, Miss Broadbent, Rev. M. Ryan, Messrs. A. 4. Jolley, E. L. Bent, W. Gill, M. W. Seton, W. Flanagan, W. C. Brooks, W. Houghton, NV. Furness, A. Bottomley (clerk), R. W. Peacock (assistant clerk), and A. Wright (superintendent of Padgate Cottage Homes).

EPIDEMIC OF MEASLES. Ata meeting of the House Committee it vraJl-deslided that owing to an epidemic of measles all children under five years of age who might be admitted to the House he immediately transferred to Padgate Schools.

-LAST OF A CURIOUS CASE. The House Committee had under consideration a letter from the Town Clerk of Warrington with refer- ence to the case of Mary E. Tompkinson. The girl was committed to Holy Trinity Industrial School, Liverpool, on the order of two magistrates. She was at the time of her committal an inmate of the Cottage Homes at Padgate. For a long time the police authorities had paid the bill for the girl's maintenance at Liverpool, but recently the Corporation sent the bill to the Guardians stating that they had been pay- ing it in error and requesting the Board to undertake payment.—The Clerk explained that the Guardians were not bound to meet the plyment but subject to the sanction of the Local Government Board they had authority to do so and taking into consideration the fact that the girl was committed at the request of the Guardians and that the cost of her maintenance would have had to be borne by them, had the prosecution not taken place, he recommended the Guardians to pay the account and also the cost of the eirrti maintenance from Ist October, 1007, IThe Comaittoe Agreed to this course which now received the sanction of the Board.

MAINTENANCE OF LUNATICS.-INCREASED COST.

_ A letter was received from the Lancashire Asylums Board intimating that the charges for maintenance would be increased from Bs. 9d. per week per patient to 9s. 4d., and that the increase would take effect on October Ist. _ _

COUNTY EXPENDITURE. The CLERK said he had received the County rate precept and the gross amount was £8,916.25. 7d. The CfrarnmaN : How does that compare with the previous year ? The CLERK : It is 670 4s. 23. more. THE ADMINISTRATION OF RELIEF. The Rev. M. RYAN moved that the resolution fixing the time of meetings of the No. 1 Relief Committee at 9.30 on the same day as the Board meetings be rescinded and that in future the committee meet at 10 a.m. on alternate Wednesdays to the Board meeting. He said his only reason for bringing forward the resolution was that the outdoor relief might be administered more effectively. One hour per fortnight was net suMcient time to examine the cases. Some days they had as many as 120 or 130 cases to deal with and it was a hardship on the Guardians as well as on the poor people that they should be kept waiting until long after the Board meeting to complete the relief business. Mr. GILL seconded and said the cases could not receive the consideration they ought to have in an hour. Mr. JOLLEY said the Board was quite entitled to expect from anyone who made a proposal of a some- what revolutionary character, as Father Ryan's undoubtedly was, much stronger arguments than had been adduced that morning. Behind the remarks of the mover and seconder appeared to be the suggestion that hitherto the persons applying for relief had not received proper consideration and that their cases had not been dealt with in a proper manner. Having presided over the administration of relief in the No. 1 district for many years, and having been specially asked to do it by the unanimous request of the Board when the change of policy was inaugurated—a policy which he ventured to think had been beneficial to the poor and to the ratepayers —he thought he was putting it mildly when he said that some stronger arguments ought to be expected by the Board before such an important change could be made. There was nothing in the sccial or industrial conditions of the district which justified the change at I the present time. If the Board wished to change its policy it was competent for it to do so but let them consider what the change meant. It meant that for a certain section of the Union area a much more lavish expenditure on out-relief was to be incurred—the expenditure not being levied merely on tho area in which it was spent but on the Union as a whole. It was much to be regretted that there should be such a small attendance of the Board when a matter of such great importance was under consideration. He pre- dicted that the increase which had already taken place in the Warrington portion of the Union would be very greatly increased if the resolution were carried. It would tend to sever the work of the Board as a whole from the work of the Guardians administering relief in that particular area. Mr. FURNFSS said they were all pleased to see Mr. I Jolley back in his place once more after such a long absence. It would have been well if more town Guardians had been present when an important departure of that kind was to be discussed. He (Mr. Furness) was, however, largely influenced by the state- ment of the chairman of the committee who had told . them emphatically that the time at their disposal was not sufficient to do justice to the applicants or to the • ratepayers. The Guardians were not justified in allow- • ing things to go on as they were. Miss BROADBENT said she did not quite see why the relief should go up because more time was given to the cases. She understood the idea was that the relief would probably be less if there was more time at the com.nittee's disposal. She, however, favoured the

3m. S. DAVIES'S RESIGNATIO'S.

The CLERK reported that Mr. S. Davies had been away from meetings of the Board for a period of over six mouths and had not given any raason for his absence in order that the Guardians might extend his leave. Ile (the Clerk) gave him particulars with respect to his position and a letter was received from Mr. Davies's son tendering his resignation from the Board. It would be necessary for two Guardians to give notice in writ- ing in order that an election could take place to fill the vacancy in Howley Ward. Mr. FURNESS said one could not help expressing feel- ings of regret that owing to ill-health, Mr. Davies should have tcr give up his public work after 19 years' service on the Board. They would be lacking in their duty if they allowed him to sever his connection with the Board without expressing their appreciation of him as a public member and of his serviceson that authority. He moved a resolution to that effect. Mr. HOUGHTON seconded and said he had known Mr. Davies all his life. Mr. BENT and the Rev. M. RYAN' supported, the latter remarking that he had always found Mr. Davies to be obliging, gentlemanly, and conscientious. His anxiety to relieve poverty and distress was an outstanding feature of his character. The CHAIRMAN having added a few words, the resolu- tion was carried.

===== 0002601_19080905_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE CLERK'S SUPERANNUATION.

The CLErtx informed the Board that Mr. J. C. Sutton, the late clerk of the Board, was appointed on June 19th, 1972, and on June 19th last he completed 36 years' service. In accordance with the provisions of the Poor Law Officers' Superannuation Act, 1890, he was entitled to a superannuation allowance equal to 36-60th's of his average salary for the past five years. His salary as Clerk to the Guardians was £3OO, and as Clerk to the Assessment Committee £125, making an average bf £425. Thirty-six 60th's of that amount was equal to a superannuation of £255. On the proposition of Mr. BENT seconded by Mr. W. C. BROOKS it was resolved : "That in accordance with the Poor Law Officers' Superannuation Act, 1890, the superannuation allowance to Mr. J. C. Sutton,-late clerk to the Guardians and Assessment Committee, be fixed at £255 per annum, such sum being equal to 36-60th's of his average salary for the five years ended 24th June last."

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

There is an interesting entertain.nent at the Palace and Hippodrome this week and it has met with full appreciation. One of the most original turns is pro- vided by Thornbury, an artist in sand, smoke and oil, who produces pictures with marvellous facility. With a few deft touches he transforms a wild seascape into a charming landscape, with lovers' walk, and he ' further evidences his skill by giving a little ventriloqual j entertainment. Instead, of producing the usual stock- in-trade, he adds novelty to the performance by executing crayon drawings of the "dolls." There are a couple of excellent musical turns. Annie Schuberth produces a song scam, "The deputy " which meets with general acceptance. The scone is laid in a music hall dressing room, and the deputy, while awaiting her" call,' rehearses a couple of songs. She possesses a sweet voice of good compass and sings with taste and expression. Shirley, Liggins and Co., appear in " Farmyard pastimes ''—a feature which has proved immensely popular. There is nothing particularly distinctive of the farmyard about it except the scenic setting, the turn consisting of vocal items which are most artistically given. All the four per- formers have good voices and use them very effectively. The Tossing Austin, American comedy jugglers and dancers, keep the audience in rare good humour, the male artiste making it his business to present mystifying tricks and then with rare frankness to" give the show away." Edwards and Were score a success with their comedy duolgue " What a life" in which a domestic difference about the mother-in- law has an entirely. unexpected settlement. Violet Nunn is a pleasing singer of coEn songs and George James is a good comedian. Harry Luck fairly Con- vulses the audiences vvith his up-to-date comic songs. I Raymond's Bio-Tableaux as usual provide an interest- ing five minutes and the orchestra does itself credit in a couple of selections. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Next week the following artistes will appear:— Gus Harris, described as the world's greatest Hebrew character deleneator in his latest success, "Tis Primrose Day," giving a wonderful impersonation of Lord Beaconsfield ; Denwartz Troupe of unique juvenile lady acrobats; Sisters Brenton, comediennes and expert dancers ; Alfonzo, feet equilibrist and tranka wonder; Little Dando, the smallest eccentric comedian on the earth; Marie Jones, said to be the most versatile protean actress of the day, assisted by Ernest Munro, presenting the quick-change novelty duologue "The way to win a bet,." which includes six distinct characters and eight complete changes; Lily Eyton, character comedienne; Harry Cotton, , eccentric commedian,

Presentation of a Cup by C011361101' Steal.

There was a large attendance at the Constitution I Lodge of Oddrellow3 on Saturday evening when P.P.G.M. Thomas S. Steel formally presented his silver challenge trophy to the trustees for annual competition in bowling amongst the members. In doing so Bro. Steel said he attached no conditions to the gift but trusted that the annual competition would tend to further cement the good feeling that existed between the members of the lodge. He favoured the social side of their membership and believed that I friendly rivalry in a game of bowls would create a lgood spirit amongst them. His one desire was to benefit the members of the Lodge and to promote a greater love for the Order they held so dear.(Applause.) The beautiful silver cup (which bore the following i inscription "Independent Order of Oddfellows (gatt-1 chester Unity) Friendly Society presented by P.P.G.11.1 'Thomas S. Steel to the Constitution Lodge (Warring- 1 ton District) for annual bowling competition amongst its members Bth August 1903 ") was accepted on behalf of the Trustees by p.p.G.m. William Daimith (the senior trustee) who remarked upon the magnia- I cence of the donor and the good spirit that had Iprompted the gift. Speeches containing many espres- sions of thanks were also made by P.P.GA. Joseph ITaylor (another trustee), P.G.F. Taylor (secretary), P.G. William Watson (treasurer), P.G. E. A. Burdett, 'IP.G. Henry Taylor (secretary of the Bowling Com- mittee), P.G. Peter Lester and P.G. Spann. A special Ivote of thanks was accorded to P.P.G.11. Steel for his gift and it was decided to play the handicap on the green of the King and Queen Hotel on Saturday the 119th inst. when other valuable prizes will also be competed for. Tile Cap is now on view in the window of lir. Wilfred Taylor, grocer, Buttermarket-street.

Councillor J. Fulton-Smith presided, and there were also present :—Mr; 11. Roberts (deputy chairman), Aldermen Bennett, Evans, Ellison, Smethurst, Coun- cillors Ball, Dickenson, Pemberton, W. 13. Roberts, Sankey, Steel, Revs. James Davies and J. Yonge, Messrs. T. P. Bradshaw,A. J. Jolley, C. E. Parker, R.T. Fairclough,E. Plinston, J.Moore Murray (director),R.B. Roberts (assistant director),John Fairhurst (treasurer). SCHOOL FEES ABOLISHED AT LATCHFORD. I At a meeting of the Finance Committee, held on the 24th August, a communication having been submitted from'the managers of the Christ Church school, suggest- ing the abolition of school fees at that school it was, in view of the small amount of the fees received, resolved that consent be given to the proposal. The minutes were approved, on the motion of Mr. JOLLEY, seconded by Mr. PAIRCLOUGH. ONE TOWN COUNCILLOR EQUALS—? The Rev. J. DAVIES, in the absence of the Rev. Canon Stevenson, moved the confirmation of the minutes of the School Management Committee, and alluded to the resolution that Dr. Donald Fergason, of 186, Orford- lane, and Mr. Joseph Wilkinson, of 13, Folly-lane be appointed managers of the Beamont Council School in place of Mr. Councillor Lord, resigned. He asked if it took two gentlemen to fill a vacancy created by the resignation of a town councillor? (Laughter-) Councillor PEMBERTON (who seconded) : I think it does. (Renewed laughter.) Mr. PARKER said it was complained that Town Councillors had so much business in regard to other matters that there was a great difficulty in getting a quorum at school managers' meetings. That was the reason the Committee went outside the Council and co-opted two managers. MORE SCHOOL ACCOMMODATION NEEDED. Mr. FAIRCLOIMI4 moved the confirmation of the minutes of the Sites and Buildings Committee, and said they were no nearer than they were at the last meeting with regard to the provision of a site in Lovely-lane for a new school. Councillor PEMBERTON Seconded. Mr. PLINSTON said he regretted to hear that report. They had just passed over the following minute of the School Attendance Committee :—" A letter from the Board of Education intimating that the Board would be unable to approve the plans of the proposed alteration •of the boys' department of the parochial school unless the accommodation of that department is reduced to not more than 600—was read, together with a communication from the managers of the school upon the subject. After carefully considering the matter, it was resolved (a) that no objection be offered to the proposed reduc- tion in the accommodation of the school ; (b) that the attention of the Education Committee be called to the shortage of school accommodation for boys in this district ;and (c) that the Director be instructed to prepare, for submission to the committee at their next meeting, a statement showleig the school accommoda- tion in the various districts in the borough." That denoted that in the near future there was going to be very serious trouble, and he hoped, therefore', that the Sites and Buildings Committee would endeavour to do what was needed in the matter of accommodation. He attended a school last week and found three large classes in one room, with three separate teachers and three separate and distinct kinds of lessons being given. When he saw what was going on he thought of the raid by suffragettes on the House of Commons—(laughter)— as a terrible row was going on, and he was surprised that any progress SVAS made in the instruction of the children. There were other schools in the same con- dition. They could not in those circumstances expect the teachers to give that attention to the scholars that was necessary, and the schniars could not give the teachers the necessary attention. The minutes were passed. MEDICAL INSPECTION OF SCHOOL CHILDREN. The Medical Inspection Seb-committee reported that at a meeting that afternoon they bad diccussed the proposals of the Health Committee for carrying out the medical inspection of school children. The committee approved the proposals and empowered their Chairman and the Director of Education to purchase such apparatus as may be necessary for the prompt inaugura- tion of the work. In moving the adoption of the minutes the CHAIRMAN said they desired to get to work with the least possible delay. The proposals would give them an opportunity to see how far they could go to fulfil the requirements of the Act, and no doubt they would find there would be a great deal of work in store for all the officers connected with the inspection. Councillor SANKEY seconded. Mr. JOLLEY Said there was another matter not men- tioned in the Medical Inspection Sub-committee's minutes. It related to the appointment of a school nurse, and the Health Committee had decided to invite applications for the position. That was approved by the sub-committee at their meeting that afternorni. It was perhaps not desirable to say too much upon the subject at this stage, but it was very important that everybody should recognize that they were running a very grave risk of incurring serious- loss to to the rate- payers by the delay in carrying out the work of medical inspection—a loss through deductions from the grants in respect of this matter. The proposals submitted by the Health Committee were, in effect, those of which this committee approved on February 17th. It would be unwise to disapprove of them now, although they would have some grounds for doing so because they had been slightly altered, and the alterations were not for the better. They made the I scheme slightly less effective and rather more expensive. There was one matter which ought not to be passed over in silence. He noticed from the minutes of the Health Committee of the Town Council that" The Medical Officer of Health intimated I that he was in a position to start the work as soon as the preliminary arrangements were made by the Educa- tion Committee, and it was resolved :—` That the Town Clerk inform the Education Committee accordingly.'" All he had got to say was that that, to anyone who knew the real history of the matter, was a gratuitous piece of official impertinence which ought not to pass without severe comment. He for one was not going, as a representative—they were all there as representativcs, MOTO or less—to quietly allow impertinence of that kind from a paid servant of that authority. Mr. H. ROBERTS Said it was only perhaps fair to say that he did not think the Medical Officer of Health intended any impertinence in his suggestion. It was simply an intimation through the Town Clerk that he was prepared to carry out the work as soon as the Education Committee were ready with their part of it. The CHAIRMAN said he did not read it in any other light than that the Medical Officer was ready, waiting for instructions. Mr. H. ROBERTS said there had been so much feeling expressed on both sides, in which he had taken his share, that be thought it was betber now to sink every possible difference of opinion and recognize the fact that the importance of the work itself demanded serious con- sideration. • The minutes were approved.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Latchford Wesleyan Church: On Sunday special services were held in the above church the preacher being Mr. Glover, of Widnes, when collections were taken in aid of the benevolent fund. Anthems were rendered by the Latchford Male Voice Choir (under the leadership of Mr. Fletcher), and solos by Messrs. Willett and Noden and the Misses Hangnail and Brawn. The sum of L 4 sa. was realized during the _ _ . .

day. The Benevolent Fund Committee have a worthy object in view in endeavouring, as far as possible to alleviate any deserving case of need which my come under notice in the neighbourhood, irrespective of creed or sect. _ _ _

' New Curate for St. Peter's Church. The Rev. J. H. Garnett. who. is lering.Orrell to take

up duty as curate in St. I3eter's parish, has been the recipient of several tangible tokens of goodwill and regret at his leaving St. James' Church, Orrell Hey, the presentations taking place at a social gathering. The congregation and friends had subscribed for a very handsome mahogany inlaid revolving bookcase and ornament, and these were presented to the rev. gentleman by a lady member, on behalf of the donors. Mr. Garnett, was also the recipient of a useful clergy- mm's pocket book and loose leaf diary from the Sunday school teachers. •

Methodist Free Church, Buckley-street. Camp meetings were held in connection with this i Church, n the morning on th 3 spare ground in Catherine - street, " Happy Jack " (Mr. J. Parr, of Leigh) and Mr. H. Woolley being the speakers. The forener related his experience of the Wood Pit Colliery disaster 30 years ago (he being the last one to be brought up alive), and also gave incidents in the recent Maypole Pit disaster. In the afternoon the service was held in Warburton-street, the speakers being "Happy Jack" and Messrs. T. Hewitt, Warrington, and J. Taylor, Cadisheati. The evening service was conducted in the Chapel and it took the form of an old-fashioned Slethodist love-feast, a meeting at which the members of the congregation are allowed the liberty of giving their experiences as Christians. The service was con- ducted by Bros. Parr, Hewitt and Taylor.

Baptist Churn, Golborne-straet. The Rev. R. E. Letheren's subject on Sunday morning was" Bells and Pomegranates" from a text in Exodus xxviii. verses 33-35. With & reference to the altered perspective in which the modern criticism of the Old Testament had placed the Tabernacle, and to the use of symbolism, the preacher said that the poets had left their footprints around these verses arid a quaint poem of George Herbert's and some lines of Mrs. Browning suggested the use he would make of the bells and the pomegranates, the one speaking of worship and testi- mony, and tlte other of sympathy and service. These I points were developed and then a third poet was I laid under contribution and William Cooper was quoted to show that the strength of the truth I suggested lay in the association of the ideas. The testimony of the lip may be golden but it will avail little unless our lives are fragrant end fruitful with sympathy and service. Worship must be wedded with work if either our own spiritual life or the church is to prosper. On the one hand one reads with fear and trembling such words as those in which Isaiah speaks of people thronging the courts of the Temple with their hands full of gifts and their voices blatant with professions of piety ; and the prophet calls them mere" temple treaders "and says that their worship is a very weariness to God, and that their i solemn assembly s wickedness, because it was divorced from holy living and human service. And on the other hand one's heart goes out to mon who are strenuously seeking to serve their fellows and lift other lives to higher levels and are yet alienated not only from the Church of Christ but even sometimes from the inspira- tion of religious faith. We shall never serve men for their highest ends save as we serve God in serving them. The golden bells of worship must be united with the pomegranate with its red heart of sympathy, it either our warship or our service is to be a success. The 'service closed with an appeal to decorate the hems of I life and sanctify the spare moments of time. We shall not save the world with our spare cash, nor with the spare moments of our time, but if Christian people will set aside a fair proportion of their spare time for doing God's service amongst men, such as the circumstances call for, they will make their own lives fruitful and fair and ring in the Christ that is to be. In the evening Mr. Letheren's subject was "Olympic Games," from 1. Corinthians, ix., 24-27. An interesting description of the ancient games was given, and various lincidents connected with the recent Marathon race were i referred to in illustration of the Christian life. The Late Ira D. Sankey.

At the Wycliffe Congregational Chui7ch on Sunday evening a memorial service to the late Ira D. Sankey was held, There was a large congregation and a number of Sankey's hymns and solos were sung. Miss Lucy Travis sang "Sowing the seed," Miss Armstrong "There were ninety and nine," and Mr. F. Brown "For you I am praying." The hymn "0 safe to the rock was sung as a quartette. The Rev. John Yonge preached from the words " With my song will I praise Bins" Psalm xxviii. 7. Mr. Yonge said that in thinking what Scripture word he should-take as suitable to his address a great many suggested themselves. Tho one just read, however, struck him as especially appropriate, because it found such conspicuous illustration in Mr. Sankey's life. Mr. Yonge went on to say that it was by no means every Christian man or every Christian woman, possessed of what was known as a good voice—who deliberately and unselfishly dedicated and consecrated it exclusively to the praise of God. Such lofty devotion of personal vocal gifts was exceedingly rare. He doubted very much if anyone in modern days had ever rivalled Mr. Sankey, in that particular. From early manhood until the last three or four years of his life, when he was stricken with blind- ness and general physical infirmity, that Christian man served his Divine Master and his fellow men singing the Gospel. It was well at the onset of their tribute to Mr. Sankey, to emphasize the notable fact that he entirely consecrated his talent of song to that highest conceivable service. Had he elected in the onset of his career to become a professional singer, there could be little doubt that a voice of such song, compass, and power would have secured him universal fame, and procured for him a considerable fortune. He might then afterwards have retired, as great singers had done before, at the head of his profession. Whether any such prospect ever dawned upon Mr. Sankey's imagination or dazzled him with its glitter and bright- ness, they did not knew. What was certain however, was, that when he came in early life to the experimental knowledge of the mighty love of Jesus, he freely gave himself to his Saviour and devoted to that Saviour's service the noblest faculty of which he possessed. Continuing Mr. Yong° said that Mr. Sankey had not much although he had some of the faculty of speech. His colleague Dwight L.-Moody had none of the faculty of song, he could not have raised a tune—it was said, to save his life, but be had or at least he acquired and developed a great gift of speech, so that he could sway huge multitudes by his gospel message. But Sankey had a supreme and unique faculty of song, and he laid that, as the offering of love upon the herd's altar, probably thereby effecting as much as far reaching good as Moody did in his preaching. What a blessed thing for the world it would be if recalled now by Sankey's , death to the memory of the signal conseoration of , those two devoted men. Others in many parts of the world, would emulate an influence which should spread I everywhere for the salvation of the world. Sankey and Moody at first met with considerable coldness and distrust from Christian people, Christian ministers, in this kingdom, but it was simple truth to say that soon they dispelled all that state of feeling by their persist- ence, their nndoubtable honesty and by their absolute unselfishness:All classes went to hear them from Royalty down to the poorest of the poor, and the worst of the bad. Our present Queen, then Princess of Wales, fre- quently heard Moody preach in London and loved Sankey's songs and solos. Archbishops, bishops, clergy, ministers of all churches were won to sympathy and were frequently in the assembled congregations. How- ever the lovers of the more stately and venerable and possibly more poetio hymnody might criticise and reject, there was no doubt that people had been caught and held by Sankey's songs and their captivating melodies. To-day many of Bankey's hymns were included in every book of church songs. Anglicans, Nonconformists as well as the more Undenominational Churches used them freely. They are sung among French Protestants and had been trans- lated into many of the tongues of the heathen world. When Mr. Sankey was in Jerusalem he heard some of his songs sung in Arabic. rhe Jerusalem, sing them and natives of New Zealand rejoiced in them. They had been heard in Rome sung by the Italian guards of the Ring of Italy. The universal prevalence of his (Sankey's) songs led him (Mr. Yonge) to make one very emphatic remark. It was widely said when Sankey and Moody were in this country that their work was a great money-making affair, and that the sale of their hymn books produced vast sums of money. The verified fact was that neither Moody nor Sankey would touch a penny of that money. The 'Times" newspaper in a notice the other day of Mr. Sankey's death definitely stated that Sankey might have claimed large copyright fees in view of the fact that so many of the tunes were his own, but he never made any such claim. The whole fabrication was disproved by some of the foremost men in London in the years when this mission was carried on, and absolute proof of the question was given. What was true was the simple fact that from the profits on part of the hymns and solos, the philan- thropic and educational establishments since carried on under Mr. Moody's superintendence in America were built and supported ; and those remained as lasting memorials of the vast work those two men were the means of doing. The collections at both the morning and evening ser- vices were in aid of the sufferers by the Abram disaster.

The Late Ira D. Sankey .

Among a number of interesting recollections of the- late Mr. Sankey in the current 193119 of the Christi(l);; is an account by Mr. W. Hind Smith of how Sauke' came to sing in this country. It arose out of a C 391131 visit paid by Mr. Moody and Mr. Smith to a Manchester music-hall in IS'2: " After an evening meeting," writes Mr. Smith. ' Mr. Moody said, When do the people spena their evenings ? ' I replied that the music-ball was the chief place. Lot's go to some of them, said Mr. Moody. I took him first to a popular place in Doansgate, where we heard a girl singing' Mr. Moody in his own quaint way, said, That 101 has got hold of the audience. I see the peopla. like to hear good singing.' From this place We went to the Oxford Music Uall, where the poopl° were crowding in after the close of the theatres. . . . As we stood, Mr. Moody said somethin4 like this, I say, Hind Smith, I see the people have good singing. I have a friend in Amerl9l connected with the Y.M.C.A. who is a splendo singer ; his name is Sankey ; if I live to cora° over again I will bring him with me—see if I doel' He did, and the result everybody knows.

MORE BETTING CASES. Heavy Fines. At the Warrington Police Court, on Monday, before the Mayor and other magistrates, Charles IVard, 6, Hume-street, was charged with loitering in Church- street on Saturday afternoon for the purpose of betting. He pleaded guilty. The Chief Constable said Constable:. Hewitt and I Holland watched the defendant from 12.50 to 2.30 and saw him receive slips from a number of men. The Mayor said the bench were determined to put. a stop to street betting, and defendant would have to pay £lO and costs or go to gaol for two months. David Roscoe, 4, Beswick-street, pleaded not guilty in a similar ofience in Liverpool-road on August 23rd. P.C. Woollacott stated that P.C. Holland and himself secreted themselves in a building in Liverpool-road and I watched defendant from 12.50 till 1.55. Defendant went in a urinal and four men followed him in and immed- iately came out. Shortly afterwards defendant came I out and a man handed him what apposied to be a I betting slip. He paid over some money to another man I and then went in the Coach and Horses Inn. In ten I minutes he came out, and three men, who appeared to be waiting for him, followed him. Defendant then went across the road and stood on the footpath. Ile there received nine betting slips from different persons. He afterwards received five snore bets, and also paid money over to three men. He then went in the Conch • end Horses, and twelve men went inside after him, and immediately came out. They did not stay inside long enough to have a tink. In answer to Mr. Steel, witness said the glass in the building in which he kept observation was thick, but )e was looking through a broken pane. He had known defendant for a long time to be a betting man, and he had not given up betting. P.C. Holland gave similar evidence, and said he could swear to having seen 14 bets made. Mr. Steel said defendant admitted that he had been a betting man, but since the advent of the new Chief Constable there had been so many prosecutions that he had given up the business. Ou the date in question he I was following his employment at Messrs. Monks, Hall, and Co.'s works, and did not do any betting. He suggested the officers were mistaken. Ile admitted having come out of the forge for n short time to cool himself, but denied having received any slips or paid money. After several witnesses had been beard, a fine of £lO and costs, or two months was imposed. THE WAR ON RATS. RIDDING THE ISLAND OF COLONSAY, OF A PEST. Lord Strathoona, the veteran High Commissioner for Canada, is the latest convert to join in the crusade against rats, now being preached by eminent scientists His remote hermit island home of Colonsay in the Hebrides, inhabited by some ninety families, has just been tbe scene of a campaign against these vermin, in which the islanders under skilled supervision laid down bait made speciallywith the contents of hundreds of tubes of Danysz virus jelly, a preparation which is very deadly to rats, but quite harmless to other animals. Every farm and homestead was systematically treated the owners cheerfully lending their co-operation. It is confidently anticipated that as a result this remote , "fair island in the sea" will now be freed from the plague of rats, which have added considerably to the , many troubles and difficulties, with which the crofters of late years have had to contend.
